# Engine Follow-ups

Open engine work: gaps, deferred decisions, harness debt and actions outside the repo. A deferred decision leaves this file in the same change that records the maintainer's answer, for RESEARCH.md's Decisions Log if the code doesn't show its reason; a gap leaves with the PR that fixes it.

Pick work by demand, not by sweeping this list (RESEARCH.md, Engineering), and work depth-first, finishing a change under way before starting new discovery (RESEARCH.md, Docs). Demand shows in the `Real usage` headings of `harness/accepted/<browser>.txt`, the failures from the harness's real-usage sample, whose draws follow how often apps lay out each kind of text (harness/README.md, Two kinds of set); By real-usage impact, below, orders the gaps by them. Most entries under Gaps matter only under 24px or in adversarial text, and the list is ordered by mechanism, not by weight.

Terms used below:
- The Chromium (or Blink), WebKit and Gecko profiles are Pretext's rules for Chrome, Safari and Firefox, picked from the user agent (`getEngineProfile()` in `src/measurement.ts`); the scans are their ports of each engine's break-opportunity scan (`src/line-breaks.ts`, `src/gecko-line-breaks.ts`).
- webkit-host is the system WebKit that installed Safari runs, which the harness drives in a background app (harness/README.md, Browsers and pins).
- The stand-in Canvas is the harness's fake measurer (`harness/invariants.ts`, used by `bun harness equal --offline`): it gives characters made-up widths from a formula, not a font's, so a result on it says nothing about shaping.
- Main before #340 is commit 6d1d2106 (2026-09-24), the last main whose break rules were Pretext's own rather than ports of the engines' line breakers.
- The old test suite is `tests/wrapping`, which the harness replaced (#341) and which was removed on 2026-09-25 (#348); its counts stay only where they carry a decision.
- The per-engine rebuild is branch `rebuild-20260916`: a from-scratch port of each engine's line breaking, never shipped, kept as the plain-text correctness reference.
- Issue #321 is the public write-up of an offline study that emulated each engine's line layout (2026-09-15); entries cite its numbered decisions and side findings.

A size such as `633 cases; 124 / 73 / 128` counts the harness cases holding the shape at b1fd05fc, then those of them on Chrome's / Firefox's / webkit-host's accepted list. Dates say when a behavior was seen, and "by" a date means on or before it: in Chrome 153, Firefox 155 and Safari 26.5.2 until 2026-09-16, then Firefox 156.0 and Safari 27.0, then the pins of 2026-09-25, Chrome 154.0.8037.57 and Firefox 156.0.1, then Chrome 154.0.8037.98 from 2026-10-06 (`harness/pins.json`); a Safari sighting from before 2026-09-16 is unconfirmed on Safari 27 unless a webkit-host case holds it.

Before starting one:
- Rules whose errors cancel land together, in layers with a replay after each: #225's broader attempts lost old-suite cases that main then passed only because two of its errors, the U+3000 hang and controls before brackets, cancelled out (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends).
- A prerequisite written here can be wrong: this file once said Firefox's breaks between styled pieces needed a model of its word segmentation, and turning on Chrome's joined-text rule for the Gecko profile sufficed.
- A fix may be on for one engine and off for another only when the off engine's losses come from a model named here as missing.

## By real-usage impact

The sample's draws inside what Pretext claims (10,345 of 11,901 cases, 87% of the weight) that fail, by cause, from the accepted lists at #397 (2026-10-01; Chrome 154.0.8037.57, Firefox 156.0.1, webkit-host), and Chrome's since the Chromium profile takes the kerning with spaces (#408, 2026-10-02), which fixed 11 of the 40 it failed at #397, 0.39%, and since it fits a word longer than its line as Chrome's lines are shaped (#435, 2026-10-05), which fixed 7 of the 29 left, and since it returns from an unfit hyphen to a break between two text segments (#446, 2026-10-05), which fixed 1 of the 22 left, and since a line that starts at a hyphen has no break after it before a digit (#NNN, 2026-10-10), which fixed 3 of the 21 left, 0.03%, table cells of two IDs and a phone number 25-33px wide, and Firefox's since the Gecko profile counts a ligature whole on its first letter where it cuts a word (#435, 2026-10-05), which fixed 1 of the 3 it failed. A share is of that in-claims weight; a count is of cases, and a rare kind of text is drawn more often than its weight. Before #397 Chrome failed 49 cases, 0.49%, and Firefox 14, 0.15%: letter spacing between joined Arabic letters was 8 and 7 of them, a ligature that letter spacing turns off 1 each, and 3 more of Firefox's were letter-spaced paragraphs that pass now, 2 of them at a spacing that isn't a whole number of app units. Before #396 webkit-host failed 24, 0.08%: 15 of them, 0.02%, were soft-hyphen breaks WebKit doesn't take (Line edges).

| Browser, all causes | Cause | Cases | Share | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chrome, 18 cases, 0.18% | Kerning between kana that Chrome's Canvas doesn't report | 15 | 0.17% | Kerning Chrome's Canvas doesn't report |
| | Others (a soft hyphen, Khmer and Myanmar words) | 3 | under 0.01% | |
| Firefox, 2 cases, 0.02% | Synthetic bold in Roboto, which Firefox's Canvas measures wider than its page | 2 | 0.02% | Canvas answers that differ from the page |
| webkit-host, 9 cases, 0.06% | Letter-spaced Latin, Devanagari and Cyrillic | 2 | 0.02% | not traced |
| | Others, one case each | 7 | 0.04% | one is the ligature that letter spacing turns off and Safari's Canvas keeps (Letter spacing) |

Outside the sample's reach: Safari 26 gets about a quarter of real keep-all paragraphs wrong, by weight (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-09-16); a device pixel ratio that changes after preparing moves emoji (Emoji correction); and the sample is macOS rendering of stand-in text (harness/README.md, Bounds and blind spots).

## Gaps

### Kerning Chrome's Canvas doesn't report

Chrome's layout shapes a line whole and its Canvas word by word, cut at each space and around each CJK character (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Chrome), so the page applies pair kerning that no Canvas width holds. The Chromium profile takes the kerning with spaces from Canvas another way (Kerning with spaces, below); between kana it follows Canvas and charges the line more than Chrome lays out. Firefox's page shapes words apart too and turns kerning off in CJK text (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Firefox), and Safari's kerning with a following space is in the WebKit profile (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges) and its page doesn't kern the kana below, so neither has the gap. Firefox does kern across a soft hyphen, where Pretext cuts a word, as Chrome does (Line edges). Main before #340 failed these cases too.
- Between kana. Hiragino Sans, Hiragino Mincho ProN and Hiragino Maru Gothic ProN kern kana pairs (GPOS `kern`), where PingFang SC and Osaka don't (16px `ファイルをアップロードしてください` is 268.80, 268.64 and 267.84px on Chrome's page against Canvas's 272px, and equal in the other two): in 16px Hiragino Sans, Chrome lays `キス` out 31.05px wide and that pair eight times 244.96px, where Canvas gives each character 16px, 256px in all, as Firefox and Safari lay it out; `って` kerns too, `テキ` doesn't, and Canvas does report a kana's kerning with a mark that stays in its Canvas word (`です。` is 46.4px in both). Chrome so fits a character more on some lines: the sample's `下人は、老婆が…` in 12px Hiragino Sans at 717.5px puts 60 characters in 716.16px, which are 720px unkerned. These are all 15 cases under "Real usage: Japanese and Chinese lines" on Chrome's list, 14 of them a break on another character and 1 a wrong line count: 9 of the sample's 192 cases with kana in a Hiragino Sans list, 5 of 48 in a Hiragino Mincho ProN list and 1 of 32 in a Meiryo list, which macOS falls back from to Hiragino. It is also why keep-all groups break otherwise (Fitting arithmetic). Canvas's default mode starts a new word at each kana (`NextWordEndIndex`, `plain_text_node.cc:93-155`, Chromium 153), so no string measures two of them together; whether `textRendering = 'optimizeLegibility'` measures a Hiragino string whole, as it does in fonts whose lookups involve the space glyph (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Chrome), wasn't tried, and is where a fix starts (the widths are from `bun harness explain` in Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-09-30; that kerning accounts for each case, from shaping the recordings' lines offline with the fonts' own tables).
- Across spaces. The Chromium profile takes this kerning since #408 (Kerning with spaces, below, has what it leaves; RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges, has the rates before and after). It fixed the 10 cases that were under "Windows-only font first in the list", "Roboto and Inter" and "Indonesian in Arial and Bulgarian in Times New Roman" on Chrome's list, and a letter-spaced Times New Roman paragraph. An 11th case under those headings wasn't kerning: at −0.2px letter spacing the browsers lay 16px Roboto's `Notifikasi` out without its fi ligature, 66.0px wide in each, where Pretext added the spacing to Canvas's width with the ligature, 65.45px. It passes in Chrome and Firefox since #397 and stays on webkit-host's list (Letter spacing).

### Letter spacing

- Cursive scripts (Arabic, Syriac, N'Ko, Mandaic, Mongolian, Phags-pa, Hanifi Rohingya) take no letter spacing in the Blink and Gecko profiles, as in Chrome since 149 and in Firefox (#397; `src/prepare.ts`). A tab after such text lands on the browser's stop with it: `name`, tab, `مرحبا بالعالم`, tab, `ok` in 15px Times New Roman at 1px and 172px, which wrapped while the Arabic was charged 2px too much, is one line, 169px wide, in Firefox 156.0.1, in Chrome 154 and in Pretext (2026-10-01). What the port leaves out:
  - Older Chromium. Chrome's rule came in two steps, both behind the runtime flag `IgnoreLetterSpacingInCursiveScripts` (`shape_result_spacing.cc:119-125`), and the profile, which reads no version, gives every Chromium the second (no version gate, by decision: RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-01). Before 138 Chrome spaces every letter, as main did before #397: there letter-spaced Arabic, Persian or Urdu was right and is now too narrow by the spacing times the text's cursive letters, 20px over a 40-letter line at 0.5px. From 138 to 148 a cursive run takes no letter spacing at all, its spaces and no-break spaces included (`? !is_cursive_script : true`): there the profile is too wide by the spacing times the run's spaces, 3.5px over an 8-word line at 0.5px, where main was too wide by the spacing times its letters and spaces. From 149 the run's spaces and no-break spaces are spaced (`!is_cursive_script || treat_as_space`), which is what the profile ports. The first step is Chromium 77ab8bb837 (2025-04-02), in 137 with the flag experimental, which is off by default, and 97e135f47a (2025-05-07, after 137 branched) made the flag stable: release tags 137.0.7151.55 and .119 have it experimental and 138.0.7204.49 stable, though Chrome's release notes list the rule under 137; whether Chrome turned it on in 137 from its servers isn't known. The second is 65df445712 (2026-04-07, Chromium #473579852), after 148 branched: tag 148.0.7778.288 has the first form and 149.0.7827.0 the second. Chrome 138 was stable on 2025-06-24 and 149 on 2026-06-02, so the older bands are un-updated Chrome and the Electron apps and Android WebViews still on such a Chromium. The bands are read from those tags' source (2026-10-01); no Chromium before 152 is on disk, so none was run. In place of one, Chrome 154 laid out the real-usage sample's 44 letter-spaced paragraphs of only cursive letters a second time with `word-spacing` set to minus the letter spacing, which takes the gap back from a cursive run's spaces as 138 to 148 do: 3 of them, 0.03% of the sample's weight and 3 of the 12 longer than a line, put a word on another line than Chrome 154 itself does, none with another line count (2026-10-01). For the band before 138 the measure is main's own failures on current Chrome, 8 of the sample's 55 letter-spaced paragraphs with cursive letters, 0.09% of the weight.
  - A rich item's text starts a Blink script run of its own, so digits or punctuation that start an item after Arabic keep their spacing where Chrome drops it.
  - A character several scripts share starts a Blink run that holds all its scripts, the one with the lowest code leading, Latin aside, which the next character that has a script narrows, and it goes on with the run before it where that run's script is one of them (`MergeSets`, `script_run_iterator.cc:491-565`). The profile gives it its leading script wherever it stands. So next to Thaana, before or after it, Chrome spaces U+060C, which the profile doesn't (U+060C and two Thaana clusters in 16px Arial, in either order: 3 gaps in Chrome 154, 2 in the profile), and next to Mongolian it gives no gap to U+3001, U+3002 or U+3008-U+300B (U+3001 and three Mongolian letters, in either order: 0 gaps in Chrome, 1 in the profile), nor to U+202F, which Latin, Mongolian and Phags-pa share, anywhere but among Latin letters, which the profile spaces (`中文`, U+202F, `中文`: 4 gaps in Chrome 154, 5 in the profile; 2026-10-01).
  - A Common character right before a mark that has script extensions takes the mark's scripts in Blink (`FetchNextCharacter`, `script_run_iterator.cc:624-635`), which the profile follows as it does a shared character: a digit or a dotted circle under an Arabic vowel sign starts an Arabic run (`ab`, `1` under U+064B, `2`, `cd`: 6 of 8 gaps in Chrome 154 and in the profile), and under a mark Arabic doesn't share it leaves one. A wide opening bracket under such a mark has the mark's scripts before Blink looks at its width, so it isn't made Han (`abc `, U+FF08 under U+064B, `12`: 4 of 7 gaps), and a no-break space under one starts the run and keeps its own gap, as a cursive run's spaces do (`abc`, a no-break space under U+064B, `12`: 4 of 6; both in Chrome 154 and in the profile). A space takes a mark's scripts as a digit does, which the profile follows since #408, whose reader of script runs reads the whole text where the one before was fed a text's segments, which hold no space: `a`, a space under U+064B, a space and `12` take 3 gaps in Chrome 154, the digits in the Arabic run, and 3 in the profile, which gave 6; an Arabic word, a space under U+0301, a space and `12` take 4, the digits out of the run, where the profile gives 5, the fifth to the mark (Characters with no advance, below), and gave 2 (2026-10-02). A mark's lowest code leads, Latin too, so the marks Syriac shares with Latin or Cyrillic start no cursive run. The gap above holds here too: in a Syriac run, a digit under one of those marks, such as U+0303 or U+1DF8, stays in the run in Chrome and leaves it in the profile (three Syriac letters, a space, `5` under U+0303, a space and `6`: 2 gaps in Chrome 154, 4 in the profile; three Syriac letters, a space, `1` under U+1DF8 and `2`: 1 and 3; 2026-10-01).
  - Outside a Latin run U+202F starts a run that is Latin where the next character with a script is, and else Mongolian, and the run takes in the characters of no script after it and, at the start of the text, before it. So digits grouped by U+202F, as French and SI numbers are, in a text with no Latin letter take no letter spacing in Chrome but at its U+0020s (`10`, U+202F, `000`: 0 of 6 gaps in Chrome 154, and 1 of 8 with ` €` after it, where the profile gives 6 and 8, as it did before #397; with ` habitants` after it, or `Il a ` before, both give every gap; 2026-10-01). This is the one shape here an app meets, as a letter-spaced price or count in an element of its own, and a port needs each run's scripts, not only whether it is cursive.
  - A segment with such letters takes no fresh-line geometry (`src/entry-geometry.ts`), so a line that starts inside an Arabic word holding ZWNJ fits by the whole word's prefixes.
  - A one-letter word exactly as wide as the spacing reads as zero-width text to the walkers.
  - Safari gives a lam-alef ligature one gap, where the WebKit profile gives its two graphemes two. It gives three Syriac letters, a space, `1` under U+1DF8 and `2` in 16px Arial 7 gaps, where the profile gives the six graphemes 6, on main too; which glyph takes the seventh isn't traced (webkit-host, 2026-10-01).
- Safari turns optional ligatures off under letter spacing and its Canvas `letterSpacing` keeps them (PLATFORM_BUGS.md), so the WebKit profile, alone since #397, measures a letter-spaced word with its ligatures: in 16px Roboto 0.58px too narrow per `fi`, 0.35px per `fl` and 1.34px per `ffi` or `ffl`. On the masonry demo's 1,904 paragraphs at 22 widths in 15px Roboto at 0.15px, 26 of 41,888 line counts are wrong and 15 paragraphs wrap again when sized to their predicted widest line; in Iowan Old Style at 0.3px, 20 and 9; in Inter and Arial none (webkit-host, 2026-09-30). Hoefler Text, Futura and Amiri ligate too. No Canvas string measures the letters unligated (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, Kerning); it waits on a WebKit fix.
- Characters with no advance take no gap in any engine, each by its own unit (in Blink per shaping cluster, in WebKit only on glyphs with an advance, in Firefox per cluster and none after a format character, but one after the last character of a text run whatever it is, `CanAddSpacingAfter`, `nsTextFrame.cpp:3860-3873`, so an Arabic word that ends its text with ZWJ or ZWNJ takes one gap on Firefox's page and none in the Gecko profile; Firefox 156, 2026-10-01): with 1px letter spacing, two word joiners (WJ) at width 1px in 16px Arial are one line in all three browsers and split in Pretext (by 2026-09-12; attempts in RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends). A mark after a space is the plainest shape: `a`, a space, U+0301, a space and `b` in 16px Arial take 4 gaps in Chrome 154 and Firefox 156.0.1, one for the space and its mark, and 5 in their profiles; webkit-host and its profile give 5 (2026-10-02). A fix needs one per-grapheme spacing unit every rule shares, with per-segment arrays only where needed; for want of it, the cursive rule takes its graphemes' gaps back from their advances. Such a grapheme with no advance then reads to the walkers as zero-width text that owns no gap, which fits wherever the line before it did: `a`, a space under U+064B, a space and `b` in pre-wrap at 1.5px, where the mark is in the Arabic run the space starts and takes no gap, keeps the second space on the first line at 16.32px, though `a` and the first space, 16.34px with their gaps, overflow once the mark follows them and Chrome moves the second space down (1 Chrome case, accepted with #408; main passed it while it charged the mark a gap, which made every line with the mark 1.5px too wide). The WebKit profile's return from a soft hyphen whose hyphen doesn't fit (`unfitHyphenRetreat`, #396) shows it: `a`, WJ, U+0301, ZWSP, U+00AD, `ib` in pre-wrap 16px Arial ends its second line at the soft hyphen in Safari at 6.33-8px, the hyphen and its gap 6.33px wide, where the profile charges the WJ and the mark a gap each and one before the hyphen, 9.33px, and returns to the ZWSP (3 webkit-host cases under 24px, which main passed by never returning). It shows at any width where such a character sits on the hyphen's line: `inter\u200Dna\u00ADtion\u2060al co\u200Cop\u00ADer\u200Ba\u00ADtion and \u2060hy\u00AD\u2060phen\u00ADa\u200Dtion` in 16px Trebuchet MS with 2px letter spacing ends its first line at the soft hyphen after `co\u200Cop` in Safari at 175.875-177.75px, 175.80px wide, where the profile charges the WJ a gap, 177.80px, and returns to the space (16 of 401 widths at 150-200px, which main passed with a line 2px too wide, against 53 gained; webkit-host, 2026-10-01). Waiting on it: a ZWSP right after a forced break inside a word, which all three browsers give a line of its own (it also waits on joined Arabic widths); Blink's shaping-cluster overflow units.
- Tab stops follow each engine since #395 (`letterSpaceTabStops`, `letterSpaceTabs`, `tabMinimumCharacter` and `tabsInAppUnits` in `src/measurement.ts`; RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts). What's left: Firefox spaces the last character of a text run whatever it is (`CanAddSpacingAfter`, `nsTextFrame.cpp:3860-3873`), so a tab that ends the text, or one before a change of direction, takes one letter spacing the Gecko profile gives no tab (`a`, tab at 1px in 16px Arial is 44.6px wide in Firefox 156.0.1 and 43.6px in Pretext; `a`, tab, `بِبِ((tail` at −1px and 26.6-27.45px keeps the tab, 26.6px wide, on the first line, and Pretext, at 27.6px, moves it down: 4 Firefox cases, accepted, whose other lines are Firefox's since #397 stopped spacing the Arabic letters; 2026-10-01). The end of the text is one comparison in `measureAnalysis()`; the change of direction wants bidi levels, which the profile resolves only where they can change a break (RESEARCH.md, Bidi Levels). Where letter spacing is minus a space or less, stops are no wider than 0 and a tab takes no advance: Gecko gives tabs no width there (`GetSpacingInternal`, `nsTextFrame.cpp:4262-4264`), and Blink's `Font::TabWidth` returns a negative advance (`font.cc:319-340`), which the line breaker clamps to 0 where it places the tabs' item (`ClampNegativeToZero`, `line_breaker.cc:1486, 1703`, Chromium 153), as Firefox and Chrome 154 lay such tabs out (792 probe inputs of tab runs, 2026-09-30). The Blink profile counts letter spacing in every stop, as Chromium does since 140, where the runtime flag `TabSizeWithSpacing` came on by default (`TabSize::GetPixelSize`, `tab_size.h:24-33`; a flag until 155): a Chromium before 140, or an embedder that turns the flag off, puts a stop every eight plain spaces, as Pretext did before #395, so under letter spacing each of the profile's stops is eight letter spacings off there. No profile reads a browser's version, and no such build was run; a report from one would reopen it. The Gecko profile's minimum is half of Canvas's width of `0`, where Gecko reads the font list's first available font, its `0` or, where it has none, its average character width (`GetMinTabAdvanceAppUnits`, `nsTextFrame.cpp:1931-1937`; `GetFirstValidFont`, `gfxTextRun.cpp:2277-2296`; `ZeroOrAveCharWidth`, `gfxFont.h:1698-1700`). Canvas draws the `0` in a later font of the list there, so under a list led by a font without a `0`, such as an icon font or a font of one script, the two minimums differ, and a tab between them from its stop lands a whole stop from Firefox's. No such list was probed. Every profile counts a stop from Canvas's space, where Blink reads the font's own advance without the tracking Canvas applies (`simple_font_data.cc:225-240`), which differs only in fonts with tracking, such as the system font. Chrome counts a tab right after a soft hyphen from a pen that still holds the hyphen the line would end with there (`HandleControlItem` reads `position_`, `line_breaker.cc:2963-2971`; only `HandleText` and a line's end take the hyphen off, `:1374-1376`), so what follows the tab starts a hyphen's width before the stop, which no profile models: `ab`, U+00AD, `cd`, tab, `ef`, U+00AD, tab, `gh` in 16px Arial at −1px is 93.16px wide in Chrome 154 and 98.48px in Pretext, 5.33px more, which wraps `gh` at 95 and 98px (4 probe inputs with the same text at 2px and 71-74px; they passed before #395 with lines 7-9px off; 2026-10-01). A soft hyphen right after a tab is charged a letter's gap before its hyphen, as every hyphen is in one number for the text (`discretionaryHyphenWidth`), and a tab leaves no gap in Blink: `a`, tab, U+00AD, `b` in 16px Arial at 1.5px and 52.9px ends its first line with the hyphen in Chrome 154, 52.89px wide, where the profile's hyphen would end at 54.39px, so its line ends after the tab (1 Chrome case, accepted; before #395 stops 12px short kept `b` on the first line, a wrong line count; Firefox not checked; 2026-10-01). Blink keeps a line's position in 1/64 of a zoomed px, 1/128px at DPR 2, each text item's width rounded up (`SnappedWidth`, `shape_result.h:170`; `position_`, `line_breaker.h:353`; Fitting arithmetic), where the Blink profile counts a tab from Canvas sums, so a tab within that unit of exactly half a space from its stop can take another stop than Chrome's: in 16px Georgia at −0.3px `rrrx` is 26.542px by Canvas and 26.547px on Chrome 154's line, 1.933px and 1.928px from a stop where half a space is 1.930px, so `rrrx`, tab, `b` is 65.62px wide in Chrome and 37.14px in Pretext (1 of 3,456 probe inputs of a prefix, a tab and `b` on one line in six fonts at −0.3, 0 and 0.4px; 2026-10-01). The Gecko profile counts in Firefox's app units, in which Firefox's Canvas gives its widths too (`CanvasRenderingContext2D.cpp:5277, 7135-7140`), so it has no such tie wherever Canvas's segment widths add up to the line's advances, and since #397 the profile's letter spacing is in whole app units too, as the page's is. Rich items count stops in their own font and letter spacing (Rich-inline item edges). 434 pre-wrap cases hold a tab and letter spacing; 60 / 29 / 37 are on the accepted lists, from 74 / 132 / 37 before #395 and 60 / 32 / 37 with it, most for what sits beside the tab: a CR, a soft hyphen, Arabic, or a width under 24px. #321's side finding 2.

### Bidi levels, direction and script runs

Pretext takes no paragraph direction and resolves no bidi levels (RESEARCH.md, Bidi Levels). Without levels:
- WebKit keeps punctuation after an overflowing first character with it to the end of its item, which a level change ends, and the profile to the end of the scan's segment (`אב((tail` on a left-to-right page; 633 cases; 124 / 73 / 128, most under 24px). WebKit also asks its scan again where levels change (`ab””tail` in a right-to-left paragraph; 15 positions; 2026-09-23).
- A mark at another bidi level than the word or number before it is shaped apart from it on the page, where Canvas measures the two as one string. Each engine cuts its text where the resolved level changes before it shapes, and takes the paragraph's level from the block's `direction`: Blink splits the item (`SegmentBidiRuns`, `inline_node.cc:1333-1436`, over `InlineItem::SetBidiLevel`, `inline_item.cc:207-254`) and ends a shaping run where the direction changes (`ShouldBreakShapingBeforeText`, `inline_node.cc:472-490`; Chromium 153), WebKit splits the text item (`breakAndComputeBidiLevels`, `InlineItemsBuilder.cpp:637-728`; WebKit 7625.1.29.11.27), and Gecko makes a text frame of each level run (below). So the page has no kerning between two characters that a level run ends between. In a right-to-left paragraph those are a Latin word or a number and the full stop, comma or other neutral character after it, where right-to-left text, a line feed or the paragraph's end follows the mark, which then takes the paragraph's level (UAX #9 N1, N2). Canvas resolves the string it is given as a paragraph of its own, left-to-right unless the context says otherwise (Blink, `plain_text_node.cc:293-362`; Gecko, `CanvasRenderingContext2D.cpp:5130-5147`, `:5261-5263`), and WebKit's resolves no levels (`CanvasRenderingContext2DBase.cpp:3064-3070`), so each measures the pair kerned. On the page `now.` is 0.82px wider than in Canvas in 15px Helvetica Neue, `apparently.` 1.11px and `P.` 2.69px; 0.88, 1.19 and 2.06px in 16px Arial; 1.04, 1.04 and 1.77px in 16px Times New Roman; and no wider in Georgia, Menlo or Arial Hebrew. Over 22 words in 33 font specs, a right-to-left block's width less a left-to-right one's is Canvas's kerning of the letter with the mark, to 0.03px, in all but 5 of the 726 in Chrome 154.0.8037.57, in all but those 5 in Firefox 156.0.1 outside `system-ui`, and in all but 4 in webkit-host (2026-10-04). The letters are r, v, w, y, F, P, T, V, W and Y before a full stop or comma in those three fonts; `system-ui`, Inter, Roboto, PT Sans, Verdana and Tahoma add f, an ellipsis, the digits (0.4-0.6px, `7` 1.2-2.0px), a colon, hyphen or slash after T, V, W and Y, and quotation marks beside round letters, and a pair the font spreads, as `f` before `)` or `”`, comes out narrower on the page (Chrome, 62 letters and digits beside 30 marks in 13 fonts). `font-kerning: none` takes the difference away in Chrome and Firefox, and an LRM after the mark, `unicode-bidi: plaintext` or `dir="auto"` on all-Latin text in all three; an RLE embedding makes it in a left-to-right paragraph; and a mark that stays at its word's level keeps its kerning, as `overnight.` before ` Ordering`. A number after right-to-left text is at a level of its own in either paragraph direction, so `בשנת 2027. אני` and `47,` before a Hebrew word are 2.0px wider than predicted in 16px Inter as left-to-right paragraphs too, in all three browsers. `overnight. Ordering now.` in pre-wrap 15px `"Helvetica Neue", "Arial Hebrew"` under `direction: rtl` is 166.13px wide on one line (166.12px in Firefox), where Pretext says 165.30px, so at 165.31-166px, as in a bubble sized to the predicted width rounded up, it takes two lines in all three browsers and one in Pretext; as a left-to-right paragraph it passes in all three. Of 60 Hebrew and Arabic chat messages written for a probe, each with a product name, a Latin word or a number, at 22 widths of 200-368px as right-to-left paragraphs in 15px Helvetica Neue, 16px Arial, 16px Times New Roman and 16px Inter (5,280 layouts a browser), 6 line counts are wrong in each browser, 9, 3 and 59 more layouts have a character on another line (Chrome, Firefox, webkit-host; most of webkit-host's are the break after a Hebrew prefix's hyphen, below), the predicted widest line, rounded up, is narrower than the browser's in 760, 900 and 975, and 39, 48 and 48 of the 240 messages wrap in a box as wide as their predicted single line; in 17px Georgia none does. A prototype that isn't checked in, handed the paragraph's direction and resolving levels only approximately, measured each level run of a segment apart: Firefox then passes all 5,280 with no line short, Chrome keeps the 1 wrong count and 4 other breaks it has on the same texts as left-to-right paragraphs, webkit-host keeps 55 breaks on another character, 52 of them that break after a prefix's hyphen, and 1, 0 and 0 of the boxes wrap. Real text rarely has the shape: 6 of the real-usage sample's 628 right-to-left draws hold a Latin letter before such a mark, none a letter its font kerns, and of macOS 27's Hebrew and Arabic localization strings with a right-to-left letter (43,151 and 44,966), taken as right-to-left paragraphs, 88 and 46 hold a pair that loses 0.3px or more in Helvetica Neue, Arial or Times New Roman, 209 and 115 in `system-ui` and 194 and 111 in Inter (counted with the prototype's resolver from Chrome's pair widths). None of the 33 accepted right-to-left rich cases that hold the shape fails for it: they end in letters that don't kern, or are in Georgia. A port needs the paragraph's direction (TODO.md), with which the decision to resolve no levels reopens (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-01); levels for a text that holds a right-to-left character or sits in a right-to-left paragraph, ICU's for the Blink and WebKit profiles and the unicode-bidi crate's for the Gecko one, whose port at 8e88756b took every paragraph as left-to-right; and each level run of a segment measured apart, one Canvas call more per run, only in segments where a level changes. In Chrome and Firefox a right-to-left run would be measured under `direction = 'rtl'`, so that Canvas doesn't cut the run's own marks off again (the next entry's Firefox case); WebKit's Canvas takes the pieces as they are. Rich inline needs the levels of the items' joined text, not each item's. Without a direction only a number after right-to-left text could be measured apart, under a left-to-right premise, the kind of half-rule that decision turned down. No checked-in case holds the shape; templates would be a Latin word ending in r, y or P before a full stop that ends a right-to-left paragraph, one before a comma and a Hebrew word, and a number before a full stop after Hebrew text in Inter, each in both directions. Only macOS fonts were measured, and nothing was benchmarked.
- Neutral characters measure in their neighbour's script: Chrome's `(` is 6.12px alone and 11px in an Arabic run. Safari paints a right-to-left `）` after Arabic at 9px, not 16px, so at 12-18px it keeps the U+202F after `中（ابب）` on that line, where Pretext gives it one of its own (67 cases; 14 / 13 / 4; by 2026-09-12). Chrome's page gives a run of neutral Latin-1 characters, such as `)` × 15 or `«»` × 8, the script before it, and its Canvas measures it as Latin, so in Amiri and Noto Naskh Arabic such a run after Arabic or Han paints wider than Pretext charges (RESEARCH.md, String Storage, 2026-09-27). On a page with `lang="en"`, Firefox paints `.` and `:` after an Arabic word in a right-to-left block narrower than its Canvas measures them, by 0.93 and 1.2px in 15px Noto Naskh Arabic; with `lang="ar"` the two agree. Of 519 Arabic and Urdu corpus paragraphs in that font at 22 widths of 200-368px, 128 of 11,418 line counts are wrong there without letter spacing and 123 at -0.2px, lines that were right before #397 only while the spacing charged to joined letters covered the difference; with `lang="ar"`, or in 16px Arial, none is (Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-01). The cause is the mark's level (the entry above): in the block the mark is in the Arabic word's run, and Canvas, measuring the segment as a left-to-right paragraph, gives the trailing mark a run of its own. Under `direction = 'rtl'` Firefox's Canvas measures the block's width (`مرحبا.` 33.70px against 34.63px), and with right-to-left runs measured so by the entry above's prototype, 4 layouts fail of the 11,176 of `corpora/ar-risalat-al-ghufran-part-1.txt`'s 508 distinct paragraphs at those widths, where 926 fail without it, 146 with a wrong line count and 780 with a character on another line (2026-10-04). Which lookup of the font narrows the mark in an Arabic run isn't traced. Safari's Canvas resolves no levels, so there the mirror is the left-to-right paragraph: `مرحبا.` is 34.63px in a left-to-right block and 33.71px in Canvas, and 34.38px against 36.22px in 16px Amiri (webkit-host, one probe, 2026-10-04).
- Chrome measures under `<html dir>` as it was when the context was made, so text against the page's direction wraps slightly differently around brackets, and by a letter's kerning with a full stop or comma (the mark at another level, above). An OffscreenCanvas whose `direction` was never set takes the page's, so on a page with `<html dir="rtl">` Pretext's widths are a right-to-left paragraph's: `now.` is 32.49px and `P.` 13.89px in 15px Helvetica Neue, against 31.66px and 11.20px without it. There the marks at the paragraph's level come out right, and the marks that stay at their word's level, as in `now. Then`, are measured the kerning too wide; Firefox's and WebKit's OffscreenCanvas stay left-to-right (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04; widths only, no lines were compared on such a page). A `direction` option fixed it in a prototype and wasn't taken (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends); it's on the list for the API discussion planned for the end of the project (TODO.md; 2026-09-13).
- In a right-to-left paragraph Safari breaks `src/|עברית` and the WebKit scan doesn't; not a case (2026-09-14, Safari 26.5.2). It also ends a line after the hyphen of a Hebrew prefix before a Latin word (`ב-|Java.`), where the scan keeps `ב-Java.` whole: 70 of the 6,600 right-to-left layouts of the probe messages above in five fonts, each the right line count with a break on another character, and none of them as left-to-right paragraphs (webkit-host, 2026-10-04; one such message at 200px, laid out alone in its process, shows it). Where WebKit takes that break isn't traced.
- Firefox splits a text run where the bidi level changes: bidi resolution makes a text frame of each level run (`nsBidiPresUtils.cpp:1037-1053`), a text run doesn't go on across two (`ContinueTextRunAcrossFrames`, `nsTextFrame.cpp:2023-2030`), and a text run starts a cluster (`gfxTextRun.cpp:2828-2835`), restarts clusters within its word and ends a word after a space before a cluster extender. The Gecko scan makes no such split (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-01), so it differs where a level run starts inside a cluster, or after a space inside one. The shapes found, as examples of that: a left-to-right mark in a right-to-left letter's cluster, as a Balinese or Batak vowel killer (U+1B44, U+1BF2) after Arabic or Hebrew letters, where Firefox takes its line breaker's break before the mark in either paragraph direction (`xx بب᯲بب yy` in 16px Arial at 42px is `xx بب`, `᯲بب`, `yy` in Firefox and `xx`, `بب᯲`, `بب yy` in Pretext); a skin-tone modifier that ends a left-to-right paragraph right after a Hebrew letter (`aa בבבב🏻` at 60px, where Firefox breaks before the modifier and Pretext after `aa`), which turns on the paragraph's direction: in a right-to-left paragraph the modifier takes the letter's level and Firefox breaks after `aa` too, so of 16 widths from 20 to 100px Pretext passes 6 in a left-to-right paragraph and all 16 in a right-to-left one, where the port, which took every paragraph as left-to-right, passed 16 and 12, and with a Hebrew word after the modifier nothing moves in either; a Prepend character (U+0600-0605, U+06DD), an Arabic number to the bidi algorithm, before a character at another level that a line can start with, as an emoji or an ideograph (`تمت ۝🙏 الحمد لله رب العالمين` at 52-60px starts with `تمت ۝` in Firefox and with `تمت` in Pretext, which keeps `۝🙏` whole; before an ideographic space, as `؀　b`, no case's verdict moves, and before a digit or a letter no prediction does); and, since bidi controls leave the text run, a mark after a control between right-to-left letters (`ب`, U+200E, kasra, `ب`). A ZWJ that ends the paragraph after a Hebrew letter, which rule L1 puts at the paragraph's level, and a Hebrew letter after a left-to-right Prepend character (U+0D4E) start a level run inside a cluster too, and move no line of 32 probe cases each. That's 24 Firefox cases of the catalog, all generated, 13 of them under 24px and the widest at 56.4px; no case holds the Prepend shape, and of 144 probe cases (U+06DD or U+0600 before an emoji, a digit or a letter in six texts, at 12 widths from 12 to 200px in both directions) the splits pass 104 and the scan without them 82. The Gecko analysis reads 7 of the harness's texts otherwise for it, none of them among the real-usage sample's 10,658, and no paragraph of the corpora (5,936) and none of 455,648 strings of macOS 27's Arabic, Hebrew and Urdu localizations; 1,672 cases of 60 written mixed-direction paragraphs and 16 as styled spans, in both directions, pass with the splits and without (2026-10-01, Firefox 156.0.1). The splits, the guard that made them only where they could matter and the port of Firefox's levels are in `src/gecko-line-breaks.ts` and `src/gecko-bidi-levels.ts` at 8e88756b.
- Firefox resolves bidi levels only in a block that is right-to-left, that has `unicode-bidi` styling or that holds a right-to-left character in 16-bit text (`nsBidiPresUtils.cpp:311-320`, `:790-828`; `ChildListMayRequireBidi`, `:1453-1476`, with `HasRTLChars`, `nsBidiUtils.h:107-111`), so `direction: rtl` alone turns it on, of text with no right-to-left character: `12`, space, LRM, space, `34` in 16px Arial keeps both spaces in a right-to-left paragraph (44.50px wide, 40.05px in a left-to-right one), which takes a level run that starts at the LRM (Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-01). It does of 8-bit text too: `(see)`, space, soft hyphen, space, `[this]` at 60px ends its first line after the first space at 40.92px in a right-to-left paragraph, where its brackets and white space are a text frame of their own that the line end doesn't trim, and at 36.47px in a left-to-right one (2026-10-01). And `\u2068GitHub \u2069 @dana more words` at 240px is 198.60px under it, with the spaces on both sides of the PDI, and 194.15px without, with one. Pretext, which takes no direction, gives one in both.
- Firefox ends a white-space run, and stops trimming a line's start, at a control that starts a level run, which turns on the paragraph's direction; the Gecko scan takes a text as one text frame (White space and controls), and rich lines carry the run across items past every such control (Rich-inline item edges, Firefox's white-space run).
- Firefox pads each bidi level run of a span that clones its edges, so a padded rich item of two directions, or of code with punctuation at its edge in a right-to-left paragraph, is an `extraWidth` wider there than rich inline counts (Rich-inline item edges, Other).
- The Gecko scan doesn't split text runs where the script changes (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-09-24). Both fuzz strings in `harness/cases/followups.ndjson` are on Firefox's list: the `ko` one (Firefox 3 lines, the profile 4) and the `th` one (Firefox 6 lines at 24px, the profile 5), where Firefox ends a line after the Myanmar letter, after the Khmer sign and after the Bengali letter. The `th` one is also on Chrome's list for an untraced reason: Chrome keeps its final U+2007 on the last line, the library on a line of its own.

### Emergency breaks inside a word

- Each engine fits an overlong word its own way (#195, #421): Chrome by right-context positions, Safari by line-start prefixes, Firefox by the advances of the word shaped whole. Chrome's is ported, and of Firefox's the part about ligatures (#435): the Blink profile fits a word of 80px or wider as lines shaped alone, and sums standalone graphemes in a narrower one. The WebKit profile takes every line from the differences of the word's prefixes, and so does the Gecko profile from 80px, with each ligature counted whole on its first letter, summing standalone graphemes below, a premise whose gap at 24-80px is measured in RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-09-27 and 2026-10-05). Prefixes are Safari's rule for a word cut once and neither engine's rule otherwise; the bullets after Chrome's have each engine's rule as read and measured on 2026-10-04, what a port of the rest needs and why none was taken.
- Chrome shapes a line of a cut word again wherever the cut is unsafe, and the Blink profile takes the longest stretch of letters that fits shaped alone, from letter pairs or the word's prefixes, with a line's first letter measured alone (#435; RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data). What that premise leaves:
  - Lines Chrome leaves wider than the box. A wrapped line with no safe offset before its end, as one whose every pair of letters is kerned, is shaped again whole with no fit test (`shaping_line_breaker.cc:498-507`) and kept if a retry one zoomed pixel narrower ends at the same place (`line_breaker.cc:4149-4209`), which turns on which glyph of a pair holds its kerning, which Canvas doesn't show, and on the device pixel ratio, which Pretext doesn't read: 16px Helvetica `AV` nine times at 39px is `AVA`, `VAVA`, `VAVA`, `VAVA`, `VAV` at a ratio of 2, each `VAVA` 39.148px wide, and six lines of three letters at 1. The profile keeps every line within the width. 105 of 17,069 probe layouts of kerned capitals, and 2 of 18,486 of real words (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04).
  - A pair that kerns apart at the cut, as `f)` in Verdana: Chrome ends the line a letter earlier than its text shaped alone would, since the letter's position in the word holds its part of the kerning (7 of those 18,486).
  - Both of those can fail a layout that the letters added up alone pass, as the profile fit every word before #435: that sum leaves out all kerning, so its lines are 0.1-0.9px off Chrome's and sometimes land on Chrome's side of a cut by luck. Of 4,480 layouts of 8 real-looking long words (a link, a message placeholder, a token, an identifier, a German and a Russian compound, a medical term, a run of capitals) in 8 fonts at 40-200px, the sum is wrong on 736 and the profile on 45: it fixes 696 and loses 5, each with the right line count and one letter on another line, 4 by a pair that kerns apart at the cut (`TY` by 0.125px in 16px Roboto, `yF` by 0.0625px in 15px Inter, `ра` and `ос` in a Russian word) and 1 by a line of nine `T` before `.` that Chrome leaves 86.92px wide in an 86px box. No recorded case is lost (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04).
  - A line that starts inside a ligature of three letters. Where the pairs don't add up to the word the profile takes the word's prefixes, and a line that starts inside the word has only its first letter measured alone: the letters after it keep prefix advances, which hold shaping from before the line's start. So a line that starts on the second `f` of `ffi` gives `i` the width of `ffi` less `ff`, where Chrome shapes `fi` again (`shaping_line_breaker.cc:304-324`). `nnnnffinnnnnnnnnnnn` in 16px Roboto at 43.8px is 5 lines in Chrome, the second `finnn`, 35.34px, and 4 in the profile, whose second is `finnnn` at 43.41px; in 16px Baskerville at 42.1px likewise 5 and 4, Chrome's `finnn` 33.66px and the profile's `finnnn` 42.01px. The sum of the letters alone passes both, off the other way by what `fi` is narrower than `f` and `i`, 0.58px and 0.17px. In bold 16px Helvetica Neue at 47.5px the profile and the sum both keep `finnnn`, 47.41px, where Chrome's line is `finnn`, 38.24px, its `fi` 0.32px wider than the two letters (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04). In 24px Hoefler Text, whose ligatures are as wide as their parts, the word passes at 62px. No harness case holds the shape. The pair advances are measured before the prefixes are, so a line's second letter could take its pair advance at no Canvas call, for a second array a segment and a read in each walker, and only outside joined scripts, where Chrome keeps the forms the word has: not built.
  - Joined scripts. Chrome shapes a line again with the whole text as context, so the letters at a cut keep the forms they have in the word, where the profile's stretch ends in a final form and its next line starts with an isolated one: 30 `ب` in 16px Arial at 100px puts 25 letters on Chrome's first line, 97.66px wide, and 23 on the profile's. A word of more than 96 graphemes is fit from pairs alone, which give a joined letter its width after one neighbour: at 248px, `'ه'.repeat(140)` in `400 14px Helvetica, Arial, sans-serif` fits 44 letters a line in Chrome and 33 in the profile, 5 lines for 4 (the letters added up alone fit 62 isolated ones). Of nine joined words and runs at 90-248px the profile passes 5 with 1 wrong line count, where the sum passed 1 with 6 (2026-10-04). Among the harness cases that pass, 7 have a line of joined Arabic 7.5px wider than Chrome paints it.
  - Under letter spacing a line that starts inside a word starts with the advance its first letter has after the letter before, as before #435 (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, has the build that tried otherwise).
  - A run of digits and number punctuation keeps the fit it had before #435, each digit's width after the digit before it at every width, with nothing added for the first digit of a line. Chrome has no rule for digits (`ShapeLine`, `shaping_line_breaker.cc:266-612`, takes them as any text), and no pinned case decides it: with digit runs sent through the profile's fit at every width, none of 43,125 pinned cases fails anew or leaves the accepted list, and 8 passing ones get Chrome's widths. What that fit gets wrong: `1` twenty times in 16px Arial at 54px is 6, 7, 6 and 1 digits in Chrome, whose second line is 55.16px wide, and 6, 7 and 7 in the profile (a wrong height); and a digit that starts a line is measured 1.19px narrow in 8 passing catalog cases of `zz 11–2b q` (`11.11.11.11.11.11.11.11` in 16px Arial at 54px passes with its second line 49.82px for Chrome's 51.01px). On 18,018 probe layouts of digit runs that fit passes 17,933 and the fit of a word 17,959: it fixes 60 and fails 34 anew, 33 of them a line Chrome leaves wider than the box, the first gap above (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04).
  - A word whose kernings cancel, so that it is as wide as its letters alone, is fit from them, as before #435: 6 of 794 probe words that add up, in Arial, Roboto and Inter (`obligatorio.` in Inter, whose `to` and `ri` kern by the same amount in opposite directions).
  - The cost on text not measured before: 7.2% more `measureText` calls than the letters added up alone on the real-usage sample, and on new interface labels 10.1% more over Chromium's 7,000 in 13px `system-ui` and up to 51% more over one language's 200 (Greek and Tamil; 17% in English, 42% in German). A pair is asked once per font, so it falls as a font sees text, from 1.07 calls a label over the first 100 labels to 0.20 over the last 3,000. The bench's `new: labels` row reads 8.3% and 15.0% slower than before #435 in two foreground runs of three sessions. No fit that asks less gives the same advances. A stop once the pairs account for the word, a floor of 90px or 100px, counting the pairs a font has met first, and the word's kerning spread over its letters, with the pairs kept from 160px or with none, were built and measured, and none is taken: each gives up layouts the fit gets right, on a premise real fonts break (RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data; Dead Ends, Fitting, Cuts And Fast Paths; Decisions Log, 2026-10-05; Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-05). What would lower it with no such premise: `getTextClusters()` in Chrome, which gives a word's positions with the word's own call, or an option that tells `prepare()` a text is never cut inside a word (TODO.md, the API discussion).
- Safari's rule, not ported. WebKit keeps the longest prefix of the word that fits, measured from the line's own start (`TextUtil::breakWord`, `TextUtil.cpp:242-365`), and gives the rest of the word the width left over, unmeasured (`AbstractLineBuilder.cpp:92-96`). The WebKit profile takes every line from the differences of the word's prefixes, which is right for a word cut once. From the second cut on, the first letter of a piece keeps its kerning with the letter before it, where WebKit measures it from the line's start, and the profile's pieces add up to the word, where WebKit's rest shrinks by what each piece took beyond its share: `AV` nine times in 16px Helvetica Neue at 85px, #421's WebKit text, has the right line count with eight letters on Safari's second line, whose nine measure 85.06px alone, and nine on the profile's, 84.32px (1 report case, accepted; webkit-host, 2026-10-05); `'AV'.repeat(17)` is 3 lines, not 4, in 36 of 36 headless WebKit checks (2026-09-15); and in a box of 1-17px a word cut into many pieces takes a line fewer in WebKit, whose rest comes to fit a line whole (3 catalog cases of `cut-words`, accepted). A port needs that width carried from line to line, which `layoutNextLine()`'s cursor of two integers can't hold, so a third cursor field, a change of the public API (TODO.md, the API discussion), and a line's start measured as its first two graphemes together. Four partial builds, one with the width carried in a prototype's third cursor field, each lost more webkit-host cases than they fixed (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, Fitting, Cuts And Fast Paths; Engine Facts, Safari (WebKit)). The WebKit profile fits past 96 graphemes from pairs (96 `ه` break where Safari does, 97 don't).
- A mark that can't start a line sends the word before it through these fits: `foo@bar.com` before `，b`, where the Blink profile split off `m` until #435 (19 cases; 0 / 0 / 0).
- Firefox's rule, ported for ligatures and not for kerning. Gecko shapes a word once and never again (`gfxTextRun::SetLineBreaks` does nothing, `gfxTextRun.cpp:1292-1301`), and a line adds up the advances its letters have in that one shaping, wherever it starts (`BreakAndMeasureText`, `:1004-1191`). A kerned pair's kerning stays where HarfBuzz put it: all on the first letter in a font that kerns through GPOS (`PairSet.hh:126-127`), as Arial, Roboto, Inter and most web fonts, and half on each letter in one with a `kern` or `kerx` pair table (`hb-kern.hh:102-106`), as macOS's Helvetica Neue, Helvetica, Times New Roman and Verdana. A ligature's whole advance is on its first letter and none on the rest (`GetAdvanceForGlyph`, `:1139-1151`), so a line ends inside a ligature only where the ligature starts the line and doesn't fit, and there each of its letters takes an equal share (`ComputeLigatureData`, `:238-322`). The Gecko profile takes the differences of the word's prefixes in words of 80px or wider and up to 96 graphemes, and a prefix measured alone ends in a letter that has none of its kerning with the next one, or in a ligature's first letter alone. For a ligature the profile moves the advance of its later letters onto its first, as Firefox counts it: where a letter's advance after its prefix isn't its advance alone, it asks Canvas, once per pair and font, whether the pair measures otherwise under the letter spacing that turns optional ligatures off, which is a ligature (#435; `countLigaturesOnFirstLetter()` in `src/measurement.ts`; RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data). The real-usage sample's `արդյունավետությունը։` in 16px `Arial, sans-serif` at 151px, whose fallback font draws `ու` as one glyph, ends its line before the ligature as in Firefox, 140.95px wide, where the prefixes kept `ո` at 150.5px, and `x ffiffiffiffiffiffi y` in 24px Hoefler Text at 15px breaks as in Firefox. What is left wrong:
  - Between two kerned letters, the line before the cut is charged the kerning its last letter doesn't have, and the next line as much too little. All three texts of #421, in 16px Helvetica Neue, are wrong line counts: `AV` nine times at 85px is 9 and 9 letters in Firefox, the first line 84.67px wide, where the prefix of nine measures 85.03px and the profile lays out 8, 9 and 1; `WAVEWAVEWAVEWAVEWA` at 102px is 9 and 9, and 8, 8 and 2 in the profile; `To.` five times at 54px is 7, 7 and 1, where the profile keeps the last `.` on the second line at 53.97px for Firefox's 54.85px (3 report cases, accepted; Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-05). In Chrome all three pass. Where the kerning decides one line alone, 1/64px under a width where Firefox's lines change, the profile's line is a letter short: `To.` eight times in 16px Helvetica Neue at 155.67px and in 16px Roboto at 163.6px keeps 22 letters on Firefox's first line, 155.65px and 163.58px, and 21 on the profile's (2 catalog cases of `cut-words`, accepted; the families' other 25 Firefox cases pass).
  - A ligature the question doesn't find stays as the prefixes have it, and the profile can keep its first letter on a line Firefox ends before it. One exactly as wide as its letters, as Helvetica Neue's and Helvetica's `fi` and `fl`, shows in no Canvas width: 18 of the 405 ligatures Firefox drew among 42,680 font and two-letter combinations probed, where the question found the other 387 and named no pair that isn't one (Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-04). One the font requires, as lam-alef, isn't one the context can turn off. A ligature of three letters is found only where both of its pairs are ligatures too. And a word under 80px adds up its letters alone, each with a glyph of its own.
  - A word of more than 96 graphemes, and a run of digits of any length. Both are fit from letter pairs, not prefixes (`MAX_PREFIX_FIT_GRAPHEMES`, which bounds the text sent to Canvas: RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, Canvas Work; `numericRunRe`), and the ligature rule isn't asked of them, so a ligature's second letter keeps the advance the pair gives it and a line can end inside the ligature, as before #435. In 16px `Arial, sans-serif` at 155px, `ու` 48 times, 96 letters, passes with 20 letters a line, 144.83px; 50 times, 100 letters, has the right line count, 5, with a letter on another line on every line, 20 letters a line in Firefox and 21 on each of the profile's first four, the first ending inside a ligature at 154.38px; and 210 times, 420 letters, is a wrong line count, 21 lines in Firefox and 20 in the profile (`bun harness explain --text=…`, Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-05; no case holds the shape). A long link is the likeliest such word, and it is cut wrong only where a cut falls inside one of its ligatures. A port moves the second letter's pair advance onto the first, which then holds the two-letter ligature's whole advance; a ligature of three letters doesn't add up from pairs. Not built, and so not counted in Firefox.
  - A line narrower than the ligature it starts with. Firefox takes the first letter and gives each letter of the ligature an equal share of its advance (`ComputeLigatureData`, `gfxTextRun.cpp:238-322`), where the profile gives the first letter the whole advance and the rest none. `x ffiffiffiffiffiffi y` in 24px Hoefler Text at 15px breaks as in Firefox, `f` then `fi`, with those lines reported 20.4px and 0px wide for Firefox's 6.8px and 13.6px; at 1px Firefox gives each letter a line, 20 in all, and the profile puts the `f` and `i` after each first `f` on one, 14 (1 catalog case, accepted, which passed before #435, while each letter of a ligature had an advance; Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-05). A port needs, for each later letter of a ligature, what a line that starts or ends there holds of its advance, which a segment's advances per letter don't, and it shows only in a box narrower than one ligature, 20.4px there: not ported.
  - Between two joined letters a prefix measured alone ends in a final form where the word shaped whole has a medial or initial one, so the line before the cut is charged the difference between the two forms and the next line as much the other way. 30 `ه` in 16px Geeza Pro at 62.5px is 8, 9, 9 and 4 letters in Firefox, whose first line is 55.82px and whose ninth letter would make it 62.6px, and 9, 9, 9 and 3 in the profile, which charges the ninth its final form's 5.15px for the medial 6.78px. Of 100 probe layouts of a joined Arabic word or run of 80px or wider cut between letters (Geeza Pro, Arial, Amiri and Noto Naskh Arabic at 45-375px), 18 fail, 17 with a letter on another line and 1 with a wrong line count (Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-04). Canvas gives a first glyph in its word's form poorly, a ZWJ beside the letter matching some fonts and not others (RESEARCH.md, Widths That Depend On Context).
  - What a port of the kerning needs, and why none was taken. Which letter of a pair holds it: Canvas totals hold both halves wherever both letters are measured, so at the text's size they don't tell the two kinds of font apart. A port was built on a premise in its place, half on the letter before the cut in every font, and was left out (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, Fitting, Cuts And Fast Paths, has it with its numbers; Decisions Log, 2026-10-05). Half is exact for the fonts that split kerning and is a premise that GPOS fonts break, which most web fonts are: there each line keeps half of the prefixes' error, and a paragraph whose errors cancelled can come out a line off (`AV` twelve times in 16px Arial at 76.8px is 8, 8, 7 and 1 letters in Firefox, 7, 8, 8 and 1 in the profile, and 8, 8 and 8 with half, a wrong height where the profile has the right one). It reopens with `getTextClusters()` in Firefox, which would give each letter's advance inside the word, or with a probe of each font's placement: Firefox rounds each glyph's advance to an app unit, 1/60px, so a pair measured at the text's size and at a much larger one shows which letter holds the kerning, at 6 to 90 `measureText` calls for a font that kerns.
- In narrow boxes Safari keeps two joined Arabic graphemes on a line where Pretext splits them (by 2026-09-12, Safari 26.5.2).
- WebKit steps its line-start rule by code point, the WebKit profile by grapheme. Where not even a word's first character fits its line, WebKit keeps the characters after it that can't start a line, testing one code unit and then stepping one code point (`firstCharacterBreakRespectingLineStartProhibitions`, `InlineContentBreaker.cpp:139-158`), so it ends the line before a combining mark or variation selector that follows such a character, inside the grapheme. The profile flags a whole grapheme by its first code unit, keeps it and goes on to the next (`getSegmentFit()`, `src/measurement.ts`). In 16px PingFang SC under `zh` at 5px, webkit-host lays `中!\u0301a` out as `中!`, U+0301, `a` and `中\u203C\uFE0F!` as `中‼`, U+FE0F, `!`, where the profile gives `中!\u0301`, `a` and one line (`bun harness explain --text=…`, Safari 27.0's WebKit, 2026-10-01). It shows only at a width narrower than a glyph, in text holding a character above U+00FF, and no case holds it. A port needs a line that ends inside a grapheme, which no cursor names.
- Where a line is narrower than a Thai word, Chrome's next line doesn't always break where the whole text's words end: `ภาษาไทย` in 16px Arial under `th` at 32px is `ภาษ`, `าไท`, `ย` in Chrome 154, and `ภาษ`, `า`, `ไทย` in Firefox 156.0.1, webkit-host and the Blink profile; with more words after it (`ภาษาไทยเป็นภาษาที่สวยงาม`) Chrome breaks after `า` too, and at 40px and 50px it breaks `ภาษา`, `ไทย`. `อาหารอ` at 26px is `อา`, `หาร`, `อ` in Chrome against the profile's `อา`, `หา`, `รอ`, as one node and as items `อาหาร`, `อ` alike (2026-09-30). Not traced in Blink's source: its lines look as if the words after an emergency break were found again from the line's start, where `Intl.Segmenter` splits the whole text once. Rich inline's breaks inside an item follow that split, so these widths can't be a browser case for them; `src/layout.test.ts` checks them against the joined text.
- Splits inside a segment take Unicode graphemes; Firefox takes its own cluster starts, keeping a Myanmar spacing mark such as U+102C with the cluster before it, though its `Intl.Segmenter` splits them (by 2026-09-12, Firefox 155). Open: whether Firefox's other cluster starts beyond ICU4X's grapheme rules (a leading extender continues a cluster, a Bengali YA joins after a VIRAMA) undo the Gecko profile's use of Chrome's grapheme table (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Firefox).
- Arabic joined across a soft hyphen is measured at isolated widths. For the Gecko profile, a ZWJ on each connected letter, gated by a check that a split's two sides add up, matched Firefox 155 on 1,458 of 1,576 Noto Naskh Arabic widths and can't reach Amiri or Noto Nastaliq Urdu (2026-09-12; RESEARCH.md, Content Language And Fonts). Prototype it with the Firefox halves of the rules that wait on joined Arabic widths, such as the ZWSP after a forced break under Letter spacing.

### Kerning with spaces

The Chromium profile adds the kerning Chrome's layout gives a word with the spaces beside it, read from Canvas through U+2028 (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges, has the model, its premises and the numbers). What it leaves:
- A line that ends at a word that kerns with the space after it. The profile puts a kerning that tightens the two on the space, which hangs, as Chrome does in centered, right-aligned, justified or underlined text and in text directly inside an element with a background, as a chat bubble's often is. In start-aligned text with no decoration or background Chrome keeps that kerning on the word, and Pretext reads no style, so there the line's last word is wider than Chrome's by the kerning, as on main, and a line can wrap a word that Chrome keeps. Of 41,888 card layouts in 15px, 43 take a line more than Chrome in Gill Sans, 16 in Avenir Next, 3 in Times New Roman and 2 in Arial, where main gets 550, 150, 71 and 70 wrong; of 46,400 interface layouts in 80 font specs 196 are wrong, where main gets 2,086 and a build with the kerning left on the word 90. No layout of either probe comes out a line fewer than Chrome for it, and a box sized to the predicted widest line makes Chrome wrap again in none. 21 Chrome cases are accepted for it, words ending in `A` in 16px Arial in boxes under 80px (16 engine facts, 5 catalog cases). The harness records only this kind of text, so the kind the placement is exact for has no case. Leaving the kerning on the word is the mirror image: exact here, and in the other kind a line fewer than Chrome (41 of Gill Sans's 43 wrong counts) with a box that makes Chrome wrap again (433 of 37,786 layouts); RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges, has the table and Blink's rule, and Dead Ends, Kerning, the build. An option on `prepare()` that says a text is start-aligned with no decoration or background would settle it (2026-10-01).
- A kerning that widens a word and the space after it stays whole on the word, which is where a font has it from GPOS. From the legacy `kern` table, or a pair subtable of `kerx`, Chrome puts half of it on the space, so a line that ends at such a word is half the kerning wider than Chrome's and can wrap early: `f` before a space in 16px Baskerville (2.31px, of which the page leaves 1.16px on the word) and in 16px Arial Rounded MT Bold (1.79px), 2 of 46,400 interface layouts, both right on main. One Canvas call per font under `fontKerning = 'normal'` tells the two kinds of font apart, which the profile's first builds asked (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges). HarfBuzz also picks GPOS, `kerx` or `kern` for each script it shapes (`hb-ot-shape.cc:134`, `163-183`), and an AAT `kerx` state-table subtable puts the whole kerning on one glyph (`hb-aat-layout-kerx-table.hh:296-334`); no font measured shows either (2026-10-01).
- A font is asked once whether it kerns any printable ASCII character with the space, by one string measured with kerning on and off (`getFontSpaceKerning()`), and a font that kerns none takes no kerning with spaces. So a font whose only pairs with the space are outside ASCII stays as on main: 18 of 819 faces measured, in 7 macOS families. Tamil MN (10 letters after a space, 1.17px at 16px), Malayalam MN and Malayalam Sangam MN (33 letters, 0.23px), Gujarati Sangam MN (0.01px), DIN Condensed (Greek `Λ` and `Τ`, 1.6px), bold Helvetica Neue (U+02BC, 0.88px) and bold Mukta Mahee (danda and curly quotes, up to 0.96px) stay wide, and bold Athelas, which moves four Greek accented capitals up to 4px away from a space before them, stays narrow. What Canvas shows with kerning off isn't asked about either, and the page has some of it: Waseem's full stop is 1.98px wider before a space, so `abc. def. ghi` stays 3.95px narrow there, and a Thai SARA AM or Lao AM after a space, which joins the space's cluster, is taken only in a font that kerns ASCII (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges, has the census and what else such a difference can be). The question is 378 submitted units for each new font, most of the sample's 85% more units, and in a font size Canvas hasn't measured in it takes about as long as measuring 40 to 100 words apart, by a background-window timing; a question of the letters, the comma and the full stop, 218 units, answers the same on every face measured but SignPainter's. A browser on the Chromium profile whose Canvas has no `fontKerning` takes no kerning with spaces, since every font's question finds none. Windows' and Android's fonts weren't measured (2026-10-01).
- The kerning is the edge character's, so a lookup that reads further into the word isn't seen, and a character that is part of a longer cluster takes none: a word that ends in a combining mark or a character past the BMP, and one that starts with a letter and a combining mark. Decomposed text whose marked letters the font draws as one glyph is right that way; a letter with a mark the font has no glyph for kerns as the bare letter in Chrome and stays as wide as on main (`x T̂ y` by 2.40px in 16px Gill Sans, `a Ṽ` the same; wide in 5 to 17 of 26 font specs). Asking Canvas about U+2028 with the whole cluster, one call per distinct marked first letter, should give Chrome's answer in both cases; it wasn't tried. Emoji and symbols of the BMP where Canvas ends a word are still asked about, for an answer of 0. A Common edge character is asked about as Common, where the page shapes it in the script of its run, and some fonts kern the two otherwise there: after Thai or Devanagari Chrome gives a comma no kerning with the space in `16px Didot, "Times New Roman"` and in italic 16px Gill Sans, where the profile takes 0.88px and 1.60px (`ไทย, ไทย`, `भाई, Yatra`, `कोच, सीट`) and main is right, so a box sized to such a line makes Chrome wrap again. For a `kern`-table font this is a HarfBuzz rule that could be ported, at the price of a script bit: its Thai, Indic, Khmer, Myanmar and Universal shapers never apply the legacy `kern` table (`plan.apply_kern = script_fallback_position`, `hb-ot-shape.cc:173-182`). Italic Gill Sans is GPOS, another cause; regular Gill Sans kerns the comma there too, and Arial, Helvetica, Times New Roman, Avenir Next and Roboto have no such pair. One Cyrillic line of 405 in 16px Chalkboard SE came out 0.63px narrow.
- A space kerns with the word after it only where the run before the space, by Script_Extensions, shares a script with that word's first letter, among Latin, Cyrillic, Greek, one class for the seven scripts whose letters join and one for all others. The runs are read by the reader the letter-spacing rule for cursive scripts shares (`readScriptRuns()` in `src/prepare.ts`), so its gaps under Letter spacing, above, are this rule's too: a character several scripts share where one of them is cursive. So a space between two scripts outside the three, Thai then Devanagari or Armenian then Georgian, kerns with the next word where Blink ends the run, in a font that covers both and kerns the space with the second; not measured. A character in one of the three counts in none of its other scripts: a middle dot is Latin and Greek here and Coptic, Georgian and more in Blink, which resolves a run of only such characters to the lowest ICU script code but Latin, Coptic, where the profile resolves it to Greek. 22 of the 213 Common characters with several extensions resolve to another class than in Blink (Unicode 16, counted with four classes). It shows only for a bracket opened in a run with no letter of one script: in `(· ж) Ω` the last space kerns with `Ω` here and not in Chrome. Text of only Common characters before the space, back to a rich item's start, counts as the word's script, where the page takes the script of the item before. A word that starts with a closing bracket is asked about as a character of no script, which shares any run, where Blink gives the bracket its opening bracket's run; not measured. Until #408 the kerning read the runs with a reader of its own, which paired any opening bracket (general category Ps) with any closing one, remembered only the last one opened, read half of a surrogate pair as Common, had no rule for a Common character under a mark, left out U+2329 as a wide bracket and took in the vertical presentation forms, which aren't paired brackets. Of 25 strings it had 10 a kerning off in 16px Arial, 0.88px, and 9 in 16px Gill Sans, 1.60px, 6 of them narrower than the page: `жж (All [All] All) All`, `All (жж] All`, `All`, `1` under U+0342, `All`, two Deseret letters before `All`, `All 〈 All` and `All 〈All〉 All` narrower; `All (жж [жж] жж) All`, `жж (All] All`, `All ︵ All` and, in Arial, `All`, `1` under U+0342, `Αλλ` wider. The shared reader has all 25 as Chrome 154 lays them out (2026-10-02).
- Text that holds a right-to-left letter or an explicit bidi control takes no kerning with spaces, since which spaces share an item with a word depends on the paragraph's direction (Bidi levels, direction and script runs, above), and stays as wide as on main where Chrome kerns: on a left-to-right page `NASA אבג דהו` by 0.89px in 16px Arial and `אבג Tom Yates` by 2.41px in Gill Sans. 26 Chrome cases of the engine facts and the catalog, `A` before a space in text with a Hebrew letter or an embedding, stay accepted for it. Left-to-right text in a right-to-left paragraph takes the kerning, which Chrome doesn't give before the paragraph's first letter, where a number or a mark is at another level than that letter: under `dir=rtl`, `5 Tel` fits 0.28px early in 16px Arial, `1 A` 0.88px and `- Tom x` 0.28px, 1.98px in Gill Sans, which main had right (2026-10-01). A `direction` option would settle both (TODO.md).
- Inside one segment Canvas shapes a quotation mark with the letters after it as one script, where the page leaves a Common character in the run before it: in 16px PT Sans `на "Android" Тест` measures 1.96px narrower than Chrome lays it out, since Canvas kerns the opening quote with `A` and the page, which has the quote in the Cyrillic run, doesn't. Main measures the word as narrow, and its unkerned spaces hid it: in Inter, which kerns no space, `[Atlas] Уфа, магазин «Yota» Тверь, офис` is 0.88px narrow on main too. With the kerning with spaces taken, it is what is left of the Latin, Cyrillic and Greek probes (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges): the 2 of 46,400 layouts and the 2 of 5,800 letter-spaced ones that main had right, all Avenir Next lines of that text or of one holding `«Wave»` (`магазин «Yota» Тверь` at 120px in 500 17px Avenir Next), and every layout where a box sized to the predicted widest line makes Chrome wrap again, 6 of 23,041 in 12 common font specs and 2 of 8,179 in 18 more, all in 14px Avenir Next, where main's box does in 11 and 0. The line is 0.32 to 1.76px narrow in Avenir Next, Inter, Iowan Old Style, Athelas, Seravek and Rockwell (2026-10-01). A fix would take back Canvas's kerning between a word's leading Common characters and its first letter where `spaceSharesScriptRun()` says they are in two runs, for the price of three Canvas calls per such pair.
- Under pre-wrap Blink starts a text item at every ZWNJ and shapes nothing across it (`inline_items_builder.cc:179-180` and `1112-1118`, `inline_node.cc:1654-1658`, Chromium 153), where the profile passes a ZWNJ over as a default ignorable, as Chrome does in white-space: normal. In pre-wrap 16px Arial `A`, ZWNJ, space, `T` is 0.88px narrow, where main is 0.29px wide, and `A`, space, ZWNJ, `T` 2.0px narrow in Gill Sans; such strings are narrow in 15 to 19 of 26 font specs, and a line of them is the one layout of 1,860 pre-wrap ones that mix scripts lost against main (2026-10-01). Taking none across a ZWNJ needs the white-space mode where a segment's kerning is cached.
- Before a pasted control Chrome gives a word no kerning with the space after it, and the profile takes it. In pre-wrap 16px Arial, `A`, a space, then a vertical tab (`aaaa A`, space, U+000B, `V bbbb cc`) has its line 0.88px narrow, where main is exact, in 3 catalog cases that pass; `A`, form feed, tab, `B`, and ` A B` under −6px letter spacing, are each 0.88px narrower than on main, which is narrow there already. Not traced. The family's other cases fail on main too, for the widths and line ends Chrome gives such controls (White space and controls).
- No Hangul syllable is asked about, on the premise that no font kerns one with the space (RESEARCH.md, Kerning At Line Edges, has what was measured). In a font that does, lines stay as wide as on main. Windows' Malgun Gothic is unwitnessed. Ideographs and kana aren't asked about because Canvas, which makes each a word of its own, can show no kerning beside one; whether Chrome's layout kerns one with a space wasn't measured, and such lines stay as on main.
- Chrome kerns across a ZWSP or a soft hyphen before a space, and across the hyphens, slashes and other break opportunities where Pretext cuts a word into segments, and none of that is taken: of 1,904 six-word lines in 15px Avenir Next, 2 still measure more than 0.1px wider than painted, one by 1.35px, and 2 in PT Sans (2026-10-01). Those lines hold no soft hyphen; Line edges has what the kerning across one costs, in Firefox too. Where a font spreads a hyphen and the letter after it, the word measures narrower than painted, on main too, where a line's unkerned spaces hid it: `The Y-shaped` is 0.32px narrow in 600 16px Gill Sans, where main was 0.32px wide, 0.24px in Hoefler Text and 0.17px in STIX Two Text, as `Y-shaped` alone is on both.
- The space between two rich items, and a word at an item's edge, take no kerning (Rich-inline item edges, below). Each item is taken alone: preserved spaces that start an item inside a line take no kerning with the word after them, as if they started the paragraph, where Chrome kerns them, and an item of one direction takes the kerning whatever the other items hold.
- A web font face whose `unicode-range` leaves out U+2028 takes no kerning with spaces: Blink tests the range before it draws U+2028 with the space glyph (`HarfBuzzGetGlyph`, `harfbuzz_face.cc:98-113`), so U+2028 falls to the next font and doesn't measure as the face's space. `x Tom` stays 0.31px wide in a Roboto face limited to `U+0000-00FF`, as on main, and is exact where the range holds `U+2000-206F`, as the Latin subset Google Fonts serves does (2026-10-01).
- Only macOS fonts were measured. Windows and Android draw Arial, Segoe UI and Roboto through the same Blink code, so the rule holds there if their fonts kern the space as these do; unwitnessed.
- No catalog template shows the space before a capital, a script change at the space, a bracket closed in another script, preserved spaces that start a line, or a font whose pairs sit in the `kern` table: the catalog's kerning cases are the ones taken from the old test suite, all `A` before a space in 16px Arial, and the sample's 11. Adding templates needs the whole catalog searched again in three browsers (harness/README.md, Adding a case).

### Line edges: U+3000, soft hyphens and closing marks

- The Chromium and Gecko profiles hang a U+3000 run that ends a segment and miss four hangs: Chrome's in pre-wrap before a preserved space or tab (`中文`, U+3000, space, `中文` at 33px in 16px PingFang SC: 2 lines, Pretext 3) and at the next segment's start after a space, Firefox's after a word joiner (34 cases; 8 / 6 / 0), and Chrome's after an overflowing letter (2026-09-23). Firefox leaves a trailing U+3000 run out of the line's width even where it fits (2026-09-24). The Chromium profile also hangs one run that Chrome doesn't: in normal white space, a run right after a collapsible space starts Chrome's next line, since its line trails that space alone and is done where anything follows it in the same text (`HandleTrailingSpaces`, `line_breaker.cc:2447-2471` and `2518-2522`, Chromium 153). `日本語`, a space, U+3000 and `文字` in 16px Hiragino Sans fail 29 of the 55 even widths from 16px to 124px in Chrome 154, and 3, at 16-20px, in Firefox 156, which hangs the run (2026-10-04). A text's run also doesn't hang before a ZWSP, a soft hyphen or a character no line starts with, as a closing mark or a small kana, where a line that fills by graphemes ends after it in Chrome and Firefox: `文字`, U+3000 and `」文` at 20-24px are `文`, `字` with the run, `」`, `文` there as spans, and in the text walkers the run takes a line of its own. A rich-inline item's last run hangs before all of these (`addIdeographicSpaceHangs()`, `src/prepare.ts`), as the walk item by item hung it; the one-node twins weren't recorded (2026-10-05). The hang needs a line that ends after the run, and none does before an item that starts with a character the scan gives no break before: a joiner or a combining mark, and in the Blink profile a bidi control, where Chrome ends the line there before each of them and Firefox before ZWNJ or a combining mark. In the Gecko profile the line ends before a bidi control that text follows, as Firefox's does.
