After a 0px box past the line's end Firefox keeps it on the line, as the paragraph does: `ab `, a 0px box and a ZWSP item at 10-40px pass all 121 layouts in each browser, 90 in Firefox on main (2026-10-05). After a letter wider than the line all three browsers give the ZWSP its line: `a`, an item of a ZWSP and `hello` at 1-8.9px, and `A` with a ZWSP at 1 and 10.65px, are 5 pinned cases in each browser that main passes and the paragraph fails, of 17, 12 and 17 with a ZWSP under 24px or at a line's start (harness/accepted). The same gap shows in Chrome where a ZWSP starts the item after a closing fullwidth mark that ends its item, at 4-7.5px: of 4,564 probe layouts under 24px around such a mark the paragraph passes 4,033 and main 4,021, and the 8 that main passes and the paragraph fails hold that ZWSP (Chrome 154.0.8037.98, 2026-10-06). The rule is the text walkers' to take, where it moves plain text too: a ZWSP doesn't hang as a collapsible space does.

What the walk item by item had right by a rule of its own and the paragraph doesn't, from a sweep after #460 (RESEARCH.md, Rich Inline Boundaries, Rich Inline As One Paragraph, has how it was made):
- All of it is rare text. Each shape needs a rare character in a certain place: a ZWSP, a soft hyphen, a bidi control, a joiner, U+3000 or a line separator at an item's edge; a combining mark at an item's start; a joiner or a bidi mark in a padded item's word; or in pre-wrap a tab in the white space that starts a padded item, or a ZWSP between two spaces. Of the real-usage sample's 241 rich-inline draws and the 17,242 paragraphs the Markdown chat demo prepares, no item starts with one of these characters, and none ends with one but a combining mark, as a Devanagari, Myanmar or Thai word can (12 of the sample's 664 items and 150 of the demo's 23,420), where no shape needs one; none of their 113 and 1,770 padded items that can break holds a joiner, a bidi control, a soft hyphen or a ZWSP; and none of the sample's 48 pre-wrap draws holds a tab or a ZWSP after a space, where the demo prepares none in pre-wrap. The counts are of cases built to hold a shape, at the widths where the two builds predict otherwise and a few beside them, so they say which build the browser agrees with, not how often an app meets the shape. "The walk" is main at #459 (e699e27e), the last build that walked item by item, behind its own harness adapter with a line's range ended at its end cursor, as main's adapter ends it (without that, the walk's passes with a form feed in webkit-host, 105 of Firefox's in gaps named elsewhere and 4 of Chrome's 175 after a chip fail by their ranges alone); "the paragraph" is main at #462 (b3128d44). A count such as 104 / 96 is of cases at 24px and wider, then under 24px, where the walk has the browser's lines and the paragraph doesn't. Three sets were recorded in Chrome 154.0.8037.98, Firefox 156.0.1 and webkit-host (WebKit 22625.1.29.11.27), in fresh documents in two orders, none with page history, and aren't checked in: the sweep's, 6,315, 9,300 and 5,788 cases of 200, 273 and 177 paragraphs (2026-10-09); a second of 247 paragraphs in other words and five fonts at every whole pixel from 12px, 30,414 cases a browser; and a third of 67 paragraphs, all but two in 16px Arial, 7,683 cases a browser, which the examples are from (both 2026-10-10). The examples that widen the fourth, sixth and eighth entries are from two more sets recorded the same way to check the entries, 12,846 and 4,957 cases a browser (2026-10-10). Over the sweep's set at 24px and wider, by the harness's pass rule, the paragraph alone has the browser's lines in 1,111, 1,652 and 1,604 cases and the walk alone in 503, 1,449 and 322 (in Firefox 1,722 against 1,371 once a line that holds only a chip or a padded span with nothing visible, which the recorder can't list, is read from the recorded height, as Firefox's counts of that set are below). The walk's are the entries below; gaps this file names elsewhere (letter spacing on a character with no advance, a padded item of white space and bidi controls in Firefox, a bidi control between two spaces, a ZWSP after a chip wider than its line); and 43, 194 and 81 passes by luck, where the walk left out a width or a break and so hid a gap both builds have: in Chrome mostly the end edge of a padded span, which Chrome doesn't reserve (33; Pre-wrap); in Firefox mostly a U+3000 that starts an item after Hebrew letters (121; Line edges has the gap, and the walk's scan of the item never saw the letter); in webkit-host mostly a form feed that starts an item (71; White space and controls).
- A ZWSP that is an item, or starts one, right after a collapsible space. All three browsers keep a line's last space trailing only while nothing follows it, and a ZWSP is content of no width: where a line's text fits and the space after it doesn't, the line ends after the space and the ZWSP starts the next line, which it holds, a line tall, where nothing follows it or the word after it doesn't fit beside it. Blink sends text that follows content past the line's end to `HandleOverflow` (`line_breaker.cc:1355-1372`, Chromium 153); WebKit keeps overflowing content only where it can be trimmed, hangs or is span tags alone (`InlineContentBreaker.cpp:160-196`), and a ZWSP is a text item (`InlineTextItem.cpp:84-88`, WebKit 7625.1.29.11.27); both were read, not stepped through, and Firefox wasn't traced. Items `Hello world ` and `\u200B` are two lines at 80-83px, where `Hello world`, 79.14px, fits and its space doesn't, and three at 39-42px, in all three browsers, in the walk, which placed an item with the space before it and gave an item that holds a ZWSP its line, and in 0.0.9; the paragraph has one line and two, as its text walkers hang the space with the ZWSP after it. So with `ab `, `\u200Bxyzwxyz` at 20-22px, the example of Line edges: six lines, the second empty, and five in the paragraph. It is that plain-text gap, which the walk covered at an item's start only: the same texts in one text node, or with the ZWSP inside the item after its space, fail at the same widths on both builds. After a chip and a space too, in Firefox as well, which hangs a ZWSP right after a chip (Boxes): `Ask `, a bold chip `@maya` with 6px of padding on each side, 68.53px wide, a space and `\u200B` take the ZWSP's line at every width up to 72px and at 100-104px. In pre-wrap a ZWSP item after an item that ends with a preserved space starts a line in Chrome and Safari and stays on the space's line in Firefox, where the paragraph is right and the walk wasn't (`let x = 1; ` and `\u200B` at 61-65px). The sweep's set: 104 / 96 in Chrome, 93 / 107 in Firefox and 101 / 103 in webkit-host, 20 of the 101 after a chip wider than its box; the second: 104 / 66, 104 / 65 and 104 / 65 of 15 paragraphs, and the paragraph alone right in none of them. 0.0.9 has the browser's lines where the item is only the ZWSP, and not where it goes on. A paragraph that holds the shape is a line shorter than the browser's for the width of one space, 4.45px here, at each width where a line's text just fits before the ZWSP: an editor's placeholder for the caret in an empty styled run, after the last space typed, is such an item. It closes with the text walkers' rule for a ZWSP, which doesn't hang as a collapsible space does and isn't consumed where a line starts after a break at a space (Line edges; Boxes, the ZWSP after content that overflows), a change that moves plain text too. The rich set pins it (`rich/item-edges/zwsp-after-space`): `Hello world ` before an item of `\u200B`, in normal white space and in pre-wrap, and `Dear ` before a bold `\u200Bfriends`, with 12 Chrome, 7 Firefox and 12 webkit-host cases at 37-84px on the accepted lists, beside `Hello world` right before the ZWSP, which passes from 24px (2026-10-10).
- A padded item that holds only a ZWSP, as an empty code span or pill that an editor keeps open with one. Chrome and Safari keep it on a line only where both its edges fit, and else move it down, with the text before it back to the line's last break where no break comes before the item: the span opens and closes on the line, and WebKit fits content one run between two wrap opportunities at a time, a span's tags with it (`nextWrapOpportunity`, `InlineFormattingUtils.cpp:456-545`). The walk reserved an item's whole `extraWidth` before its text, which is that fit. The paragraph gives an item that opens with a ZWSP a start edge of its own and fits it as the opening of an item that goes on (`getOpeningFit()`, `src/rich-inline.ts`), by half the `extraWidth` in the Blink and WebKit profiles, so for the width of the end edge it keeps the item on the line and reports a line wider than the width given. `click here ` and `\u200B` with 4px of padding on each side are `click here`, 67.58px, and the span alone, 8px, at 77-80px in Chrome and webkit-host, and one line of 80.02px in the paragraph; `Add tag` and `\u200B` with 5px are `Add` / `tag` with the span at 61-65px, and one line of 65.16px. Firefox passes on both builds, as the Gecko profile fits both edges of every opening. A padded item of a space and a ZWSP is the entry above inside a padded item, in all three browsers: `on` and an item ` \u200B` with 4px are two lines at 18-30px, the item alone on the second, and one in the paragraph. A padded item that starts with a ZWSP and goes on is fit by its start edge in Safari, as the paragraph has it and the walk didn't (`Tag:`, `\u200Bvalue` with 4px and ` and more` at 35-38px). The sweep's set: 90 / 69 in Chrome and 79 / 72 in webkit-host, with 7 / 3 and 29 / 16 the other way, where the item goes on; in Firefox 65 / 105, each an item that starts with a space and a ZWSP. The second set: 78 / 42 in Chrome and in webkit-host for an item of only a ZWSP, none in Firefox, and 54 / 53, 56 / 60 and 74 / 59 with the space. 0.0.9 has the browser's lines where a space comes before the item; where none does it ends the line right before the item at some widths (`Add tag` / the span at 61-65px). A port fits both edges of an item that is only its opening, as `'placed'` does for one of only white space (`paddedOpeningFit`, `src/measurement.ts`). The rich set pins it (`rich/item-edges/padded-zwsp`): `click here ` and `I typed` before the padded `\u200B`, with 3 Chrome and 3 webkit-host cases at 56-80px accepted; `on` before the padded ` \u200B`, with 4 cases at 17-31px accepted in each browser; and `Tag:` before a padded `\u200BNEW`, which passes (2026-10-10).
- After a chip or a box wider than its line, an item that starts with a character the line-break rules keep with what stands before it: a bidi control, ZWNJ or a combining mark. Chrome ends the chip's line right after the chip (`CanBreakAfterAtomicInline`, `line_breaker.cc:1168-1183`, Chromium 153) and looks for the next line's breaks from that line's start, without the text before it (`break_iterator_.SetStartOffset`, `:550`), so the character starts the next line and stays with the letters after it: where the word doesn't fit, it is cut between letters and the character's line holds the first of them. A chip `blue`, 30.25px wide, before an item `\u200Esunlight` is `blue` / `su` / `nli` / `ght` at 23-24px in Chrome, and five lines in the paragraph, the second the control alone, 0px wide: one scan of the joined text gives a break after the control, as after any character with marks (UAX #14 LB9), which is Chrome's too where the chip fits and the control stays on its line, and a break too many where the control starts a line. The walk scanned each item's text from its own start, which is Chrome's scan wherever the item starts a line and not where it continues one, where the paragraph is the one that is right (this section's first paragraph; in the second set an ideograph wider than its line before such an item is the walk's in 12 cases and the paragraph's in 179). Firefox lays ZWNJ and a combining mark out the same way, as its line breaker starts again at an atomic frame (RESEARCH.md, Rich Inline As One Paragraph; not traced for this shape), and drops a bidi control, where both builds pass; ZWJ and a word joiner, which give no break after them, differ in no browser, and Safari in nothing. The sweep's set: 175 / 199 in Chrome and 127 / 127 in Firefox, there each with ZWNJ or U+0301; the second: in Chrome 320 / 95 with a bidi control and 167 / 81 with ZWNJ or a mark, in Firefox 167 / 81 with ZWNJ or a mark, and the paragraph alone right in none of those; a padded item of only a control after a chip goes the paragraph's way in Chrome, which keeps it on the chip's line (34 / 13 of the sweep's cases). 0.0.9 gives a bidi control or a ZWNJ that starts an item its own line too, and has the browser's lines with a combining mark, or with the character in an item of its own. It is the cause Small ones names, Blink's ICU pass started again at each line, here at an item's edge: in one text node both builds fail it (`。`, U+200E and `on` at 13px are three lines in Chrome and four predicted). A port starts the Gecko scan again after an object, and gives the Blink profile's walker a line start that takes such a character with the segment after it. The rich set pins it (`rich/item-edges/control-after-chip`): a chip `@international-team` before `\u2068documentation\u2069` and before `\u200Cdocumentation`, and the isolate after a chip `@bob`, narrower than the word, where the paragraph has the browsers' lines wherever the chip fits; 10 Chrome and 4 Firefox cases at 8.9-101px are accepted (2026-10-10).
- A padded item whose word holds a joiner or a bidi mark, at the item's start or inside it, where a line cuts the word beside that character. Firefox fits each line of the span with both its edges; Chrome paints both on every line and fits the start edge, with the end edge only where the span ends on the line (Pre-wrap has Chrome's fit). The walk charged an item's `extraWidth` on every piece. The paragraph leaves it out of a line that starts at that character, or at the letter right before it: a line that starts inside a word at a stretch that holds a default-ignorable character, as a joiner or a bidi mark, takes its widths from the text measured afresh from there (`entryGeometry`, `src/entry-geometry.ts`, in the desktop Blink and Gecko profiles), and the walkers' branch for such a start (`walkPreparedComplexLines()` and `stepPreparedSimpleLineGeometry()`, `src/line-break.ts`) doesn't add the item's `extraWidth`, which their branch for any other start inside a word does (`insideExtras`). So that line holds a letter more than the browser's, and it and its fragment are reported an `extraWidth` narrower than the browser paints them. `inter` and an item `\u200Doperability` with 4px of padding on each side are `inter` / `oper` / `abilit` / `y` at 41-48px in Firefox and at 41-44px in Chrome, each piece 8px wider than its text, and `inter` / `opera` / `bility` in the paragraph, whose second line is 40.92px of text. An item `inter\u200Coperability` with that padding, alone in its paragraph, is `inte` / `r\u200Cop` / `era` / `bilit` / `y` at 34-39px in Firefox and at 34-35px in Chrome, and four lines in the paragraph, whose second, `r\u200Cope`, is 32.03px of text at 34-37px; after `a ` it fails at the same widths. At the item's start the two builds differ in Firefox with ZWJ, ZWNJ and a word joiner, and not with a bidi control, where both have the same lines; inside the item, with ZWJ, ZWNJ and U+200E. In Chrome they differ with all four at the start and with ZWJ and ZWNJ inside, though outside those pixels both builds fail the paragraph, with the character or without, by the end edge Chrome doesn't reserve. A padded item of only a bidi control is a box as wide as its padding where it stands in Firefox, in pre-wrap and in normal white space. In the Gecko profile the control is a segment that holds the item's `extraWidth`, with no break after it, so the padding goes to the line of the word after it, and a control that ends the paragraph has no segment and takes no room (Other has the item of a space and a control). `東`, `\u200E` with 4px of padding and `東京` are `東` with the span / `東京` at 32-39px in Firefox and in the walk, and three lines in the paragraph, the second 24px for `東`; `東京` and that item are `東` / `京` with the span at those widths, and one line of 32px in the paragraph; in pre-wrap `a `, `\u200E` with 6px of padding and `bbbb cc` are `a ` with the span / `bbb` / `b cc` at 30-35px, and four lines in the paragraph, the second 29.80px for `bb`. After a collapsible space both builds put the padding with the next word, and Chrome and Safari pass on both. The sweep's set: in Firefox 90 / 6 of the first shape and 219 / 22 of the second, 107 / 2 of them in normal white space, and 24 / 27 in Chrome, where 152 / 145 more fail on both builds; the second set: 39 / 21 and 82 / 10 in Firefox, 16 of the 82 in normal white space, and 8 / 0 in Chrome. 0.0.9 has the browser's lines in the first shape; in the second, in normal white space, it has Firefox's where an ideograph follows the item and not where the item ends the paragraph, and its rich inline has no pre-wrap. The first shape should close by adding the item's `extraWidth` in the walkers' branch for a word measured afresh, which isn't built: it is the design's own rule (RESEARCH.md, Rich Inline As One Paragraph), and no trade on these sets, where the paragraph alone is right in 4 / 0 of Chrome's cases and 4 / 2 of Firefox's; as a change to the walkers it wants the gate and the bench. A port of the second gives the Gecko profile a break after a padded item of only bidi controls where the text has one there, and a segment for such an item that ends the paragraph; Firefox's source wasn't traced for it. The rich set pins both: `rich/item-edges/padded-joiner-start`, `inter` before a padded `\u200Coperability` beside the same item without the ZWNJ, with 2 Firefox cases at 44-48px and 1 Chrome case at 44px accepted, and 6 more of Chrome's by the end edge it doesn't reserve; and `rich/pre-wrap/padded-bidi-control`, `a `, a padded `\u200E` and `bbbb cc` beside the control with the first `b` in the padded item, with 2 Firefox cases at 58-61px accepted (2026-10-10).
- A padded item that starts with a soft hyphen, as a styled run that starts at a syllable's edge of text hyphenated ahead of time. A line that ends at that soft hyphen holds the item's first piece, its hyphen: Firefox fits the piece with both edges, Chrome and Safari with its start edge, and all three paint both. The walk reserved the whole `extraWidth` there. The paragraph puts an item's `extraWidth` on its first segment that takes room, which comes after the soft hyphen, so the hyphen's line fits and reports no padding. Of `a a` and an item `\u00ADa` with 4px of padding on each side, the line `a a-` is 27.57px of text and hyphen, 31.57px with the start edge and 35.57px as painted. Firefox ends the first line there from 36px, as the walk did, Chrome and webkit-host from 32px, the end edge past the line, and the paragraph from 28px. So at 28-31px the walk has every browser's lines and the paragraph none's; at 32-35px the walk has Firefox's and the paragraph Chrome's and Safari's; and wherever a line ends at that hyphen the paragraph reports it an `extraWidth` narrower than it paints, 27.57px for 35.57px, the kind of width that clips a box sized to it. Where no break comes before the word and the hyphen's line doesn't fit, the browsers cut the word between letters or give the hyphen's piece a line, and neither build has that. After an ideograph Firefox paints no hyphen there and both builds pass. The sweep's set, the walk's cases and then the paragraph's at 24px and wider: 43 and 126 in Chrome, 84 and 93 in Firefox (88 and 89 by the harness's rule) and 49 and 127 in webkit-host, where most of the paragraph's are another shape it has right, a padded item of only a soft hyphen and a space (80 in Chrome, 89 in Firefox, 80 in webkit-host), which leaves 43 against 46 in Chrome and 49 against 47 in webkit-host. The third set, three paragraphs with a break before the word: 13 and 13 in Chrome, 18 and none in Firefox, 13 and 18 in webkit-host. In the second set, of the 387, 366 and 387 cases at 24px and wider where both builds end the same line at that hyphen, the walk reports the line as wide as the browser paints it in 273, 297 and 362 and the paragraph in 15, 0 and 0. 0.0.9 ended such a line at the item's edge without the hyphen, which it neither painted nor fitted (`a a`, 22.24px, from 23px), so its lines and that width were wrong too. A port charges the first piece as each engine fits it, both edges in the Gecko profile and the start edge in the others, which `extraWidth` doesn't split off (TODO.md, the API discussion); the whole `extraWidth` in every profile is the walk's line again, a trade in Chrome and Safari and the painted width in all three. The rich set pins it (`rich/item-edges/padded-soft-hyphen-start`): `a a` before a padded `\u00ADa` and `see photo` before a padded `\u00ADgraphy`, with 3 cases accepted in each browser, at 29-81px in Chrome and webkit-host and at 31-86px in Firefox, beside `a a\u00AD` before a padded `a`, which passes from 24px (2026-10-10).
- In pre-wrap, a preserved space, a ZWSP in that space's item and another preserved space, in the same item or at the next one's start. Firefox hangs the first space and starts the next line with the ZWSP, so the second space is on that line: `wait \u200B` and ` here now` are `wait ` / ` ` / `her` and three more lines at 29px, two more at 30-32px, and `ab \u200B` and ` cd` three lines at 18-21px, as are `ab \u200B c` before `d` and `a` before `b \u200B cd`, with no item edge at the ZWSP; `wait `, `for it \u200B here` and ` now` are six lines at 32px and five at 33-35px, five and four in the paragraph. The walk ended the run of spaces that hang at the ZWSP, as the text walkers do inside one text: the same text in one text node, or as a paragraph of one item without `extraWidth`, passes on both builds. In a paragraph of several items, or of one with `extraWidth`, the Gecko profile carries the run on past a zero-width break (`hangGoesOnPastEmpty`, `src/line-break.ts`), as past the one its scan makes of a soft hyphen there, which Firefox drops (Boxes), so both spaces hang on the first line, a line fewer. That is Firefox's layout where the ZWSP starts an item or is one (`ab ` and `\u200B cd` are two lines at 18-21px), where the walk was the one that was wrong. Chrome and Safari start the line with the ZWSP as well, and their profiles have it on both builds. The sweep's set: 30 / 27 in Firefox; the second: 34 / 28, each with the ZWSP at its item's end. Before an item of a line feed in place of the space, all three browsers give the ZWSP a line, as the walk did by luck (58 / 37 more of the sweep's Firefox cases; 17 / 19, 18 / 19 and 18 / 19 of the second set's in the three browsers): its second line came from the line feed standing in an item of its own, and with the line feed in the ZWSP's item, or nothing after the ZWSP, both builds are a line short, by the gap of the ZWSP after a space, above. 0.0.9's rich inline has no pre-wrap. A port ends the Gecko profile's run at a ZWSP that follows the space in its own item and carries it on past one that starts an item: the walker's test reads the segment's kind, which a ZWSP shares with the zero-width break the Gecko scan makes of a soft hyphen. The rich set pins it (`rich/pre-wrap/zwsp-ends-hanging-spaces`): `Please wait \u200B` before ` now`, with 3 Firefox cases at 81.8-84px and one at width 1 accepted, beside the same without the ZWSP, where Firefox's cases all pass (2026-10-10).
- In pre-wrap, a padded item that starts with a tab, or with spaces and a tab. Firefox doesn't hang a tab and gives no break inside a run of spaces and tabs (`nsLineBreaker.cpp:318-330`), and nothing parts a span's start edge from its first content, so such a span stays whole where it starts a line, however far the tab overflows, and after text the line goes back to the break before the run. The walk had both (its `runsIntoTab` and `openingEdge`). The paragraph gives an item that opens with a tab a start edge of its own, a segment a line can end after: a span of only a tab with 4px of padding on each side is one line at every width in Firefox and two up to 39px in the paragraph, the first the edge alone, 8px wide; `ab` before a padded span of a tab and `cd` is four lines at 18-24px and five in the paragraph; and `a b c`, a padded span of a space, a tab and `d`, and ` e` is `a b ` / `c ` / the tab / `d ` / `e` at 23-30px, where the paragraph ends the second line before the span's space and gives the edge and that space a line, and `a b ` / `c ` with the tab / `d e` at 43-48px, where the paragraph keeps `c` on the first line. The sweep's set: 152 / 107 in Firefox, 15 of the 152 by luck, in 13px Menlo spans whose tab fits Firefox's line and not the item's own stop (Pre-wrap); the second: 194 / 85, the paragraph alone right in none. Without padding, or with the tab inside the item, nothing differs, and in Safari the paragraph's fit of such an opening is the gain Pre-wrap names. 0.0.9's rich inline has no pre-wrap. A port gives the Gecko profile no start edge for an opening that runs into a tab: the tab's segment holds the `extraWidth`, as a letter's would. The rich set pins it (`rich/pre-wrap/tab-starts-padded-item`): `a b c` before a padded item of a space, a tab and `d`, and a padded tab and `end` in 16px Courier New, with 11 Firefox cases at 1-75.2px accepted, beside the first without its padding, where Firefox's cases all pass (2026-10-10).
- A run of U+3000 that ends an item, before an item that starts with a bidi control, a joiner or a combining mark. Firefox drops the control and hangs the run wherever the line ends there; Chrome hangs it and starts the next line with the control, which holds that line where nothing visible follows. The walk hung an item's last run whatever followed. The paragraph gives the run its hang (`addIdeographicSpaceHangs()`, `src/prepare.ts`), and no line can end after it before a character the scan gives no break before, so there a line the run overflows goes back to its last break: in the Blink profile before a bidi control, a joiner or a combining mark, and in the Gecko profile before a joiner or a combining mark. `大阪市` with U+3000, `\u200F` and `次` are `大阪市` / `次` at 48-63px in Chrome and in Firefox, and `大阪` / `市`, the run and `次` in the Blink profile, where the walk had Chrome's lines; the Gecko profile has Firefox's, the control starting the second line, where the walk didn't, and with ZWNJ or U+0301 in the control's place it is the profile that goes back and the walk that had Firefox's lines. With nothing after the control (`東京都` with U+3000, then `\u200E`) Chrome has `東京都` and a line of the control, the walk one line and the paragraph `東京` / `都` with the run, and where a line holds one ideograph the paragraph ends it after the run and gives the control the next, which is Chrome's layout and a line more than the walk's. In the Gecko profile a control that ends the paragraph is kept with the run's segment, whose text then doesn't end with the run, so the run gets no hang where only such characters follow it: `東京都` with U+3000 and `\u200E` are one line at 48-63px in Firefox and `東京` / `都` with the run in the profile, and three lines at 16-31px for the profile's four, the run alone on the last; the same text in one text node fails at those widths on both builds (Line edges). The sweep's set: 167 / 83 in Firefox, with 106 / 18 the other way, 98 / 4 of them where text follows the run in a later item, the gain this section names above, and the rest at an isolate's opening control or in a box narrower than an ideograph; in Chrome none for the walk and 166 / 79 for the paragraph. The second set: 219 / 54 against 26 / 23 in Firefox; in Chrome 15 / 0 for the walk, each with text after the control, 82 / 58 for the paragraph and 208 / 62 that both have wrong. 0.0.9 has none of the walk's. A port reads the run past the characters Firefox drops in the Gecko profile, which moves plain text too, and ends the line after an item's last run where the next item starts with such a character, in the Blink profile and, before ZWNJ or a combining mark, in the Gecko profile. The rich set pins it (`rich/item-edges/ideographic-space-before-control`): `東京都` with U+3000 before `\u200E`, with 3 Firefox cases at 16-48px and 3 Chrome cases at 48-64px accepted; `大阪市` with U+3000 before `\u200F次`, with 1 Chrome case at 48.01px accepted, where Firefox's all pass; and `次` right after the run, which passes from 16px (2026-10-10).
- By a line's width alone: in Safari a line that ends at U+2028 right after a collapsible space, across an item's edge, is as wide as its text, as the walk reported it, and a space wider in the paragraph, which counts the space of a text that ends at a hard break as plain text does (Line edges has the plain-text gap and the fix that was measured for it). `Hello `, `\u2028` and `world again` report a first line of 40.91px for Safari's 36.46px at every width from 37px, so past the width given at 37-40px. In the sweep's set the walk has Safari's widths in all 206 / 2 such cases with the separator beside a space at an item's edge, and in 70 / 12 more where the separator is a padded item, and the paragraph in none; in the second set in 1,299 cases at 24px and wider, and in the third at every width from 24px of two paragraphs, 342 cases. No line moves but in one paragraph of the sweep, 5 cases at 83-87px, where a padded separator after a space stays on its line in Safari. In lines the shape goes the paragraph's way: after a chip wider than its box, a separator that starts the next item after a space gets no line of its own in Safari (122 / 91 of the sweep's cases).

Other:
- A box narrower than 1px shows the gaps rich inline has at every size, which its line functions' floor of 1px hid until #462 (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-09, rich inline's width). On a probe that isn't checked in (609 paragraphs at 0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 and 1px: 560 with content narrower than 1px, which is spans in 0.5px, 1px and 2px Arial, boxes and padding of 1px and less and letter spacing as negative as the letters are wide, and 49 of only 16px text, 3px boxes and items of no width), rich inline at the width given has the browser's lines on 1,734 of the 2,860 layouts with something visible in Chrome, 2,042 of 2,757 in Firefox and 2,045 of 2,840 in webkit-host, where the floor had them on 1,149, 1,244 and 1,238; 55, 40 and 13 layouts that the floor had right are wrong at the width given (Chrome 154.0.8037.98, Firefox 156.0.1, WebKit 22625.1.29.11.27, 2026-10-09). Of those, 10 in each browser are right by the recorded height: they hold a line of only an atomic item of white space, which the recorder can't list (harness/README.md, Bounds and blind spots). The other 45, 30 and 3 are wrong the same way on main at 16 times the size, with every font size, letter spacing, box, padding and width times 16, a width of 0 as 1px, where no floor applied: the browser has the same lines there, and main the lines rich inline now has at the small size (one of webkit-host's 3 breaks its last word otherwise at the two sizes, in Safari and in rich inline alike, by the 1/64px a line may overflow there). At 1px the floor held content that overflows a narrower box, and where nothing overflows these gaps don't show. What the larger paragraphs show, none of it traced in an engine's source:
  - In pre-wrap, Chrome keeps preserved spaces on the line of a letter that overflows it (Negative letter spacing and hanging spaces): `a`, two spaces and `b` in 16px Arial at 1, 4 and 8px are `a` with the spaces and then `b` in Chrome, where Firefox, webkit-host and the text walkers give the spaces the line between.
  - In pre-wrap, preserved spaces after a padded span of only a ZWSP stay on the span's line in Chrome, and in Safari from the width of the span's start edge, where rich inline starts the next line with them, as Firefox does: a ZWSP with 8px of padding on each side before a span of two spaces in 16px Arial is one line at 1, 4, 8 and 12px in Chrome and at 8 and 12px in webkit-host, and two in rich inline.
  - In pre-wrap, Firefox doesn't break a word between letters after an item of only a soft hyphen that starts the paragraph: a span of U+00AD before a span of `iiii` in 16px Arial, 14.2px wide, is one line at 1, 4, 8 and 12px in Firefox, where rich inline breaks the word between letters, as Chrome and Safari do after the line they give that soft hyphen (Line edges: U+3000, soft hyphens and closing marks).
  - Firefox gives a word joiner after a box wider than the line no line of its own, where Chrome, Safari and every profile do: a 16px box, a word joiner and a 4px box in pre-wrap at 1px are two lines in Firefox and three in the others and in rich inline, the word joiner alone on the second; and in normal white space a 16px box, a word joiner and an atomic item of only a ZWSP, after a padded span of a ZWSP, share the box's line at 1 to 12px in Firefox, where the others and rich inline start a line with the word joiner. So do two ZWSPs and an atomic item of only a ZWSP after a 4px box at 1px, one line in Firefox and two in the others and in rich inline.
  - Text narrower than nothing, under letter spacing more negative than its letters are wide, stays whole on the line it follows in Chrome though its first letter doesn't fit there, and in Firefox on the line of a box or a preserved space that overflows, where the text walkers end the line before it: an 8px box and `a b` at −137.6px letter spacing in 256px Arial share a line at 8 and 12px in Chrome, and in pre-wrap a 16px box and `iiii` at −57.6px share one at 1 to 12px in Firefox, as do a space in 16px Arial and that `iiii` at 1 and 4px (Negative letter spacing and hanging spaces has Chrome's rule for an item that fits whole).
- A bidi control alone in its item holds a line in the Gecko profile where the character after it is wider than the line: the control is a segment of its own, of no width, and a line that starts with it ends before a letter that doesn't fit, where Firefox, which drops the control, starts the line with that letter. `see `, an item of U+200E and `word` in 16px Arial are seven lines in Firefox 156.0.1 at 1-11px and eight in the profile, the control alone on the fourth; with the control at an item's start or end, or in one text node, the profile has Firefox's lines, and Chrome and Safari give the control that line, as their profiles do. It shows only in a box narrower than that character: of 2,880 probe layouts with each of the twelve controls alone in its item, at 40 widths from 2px to 130px, 192 fail, at 2, 4 and 8px (2026-10-09 and 10, a probe that isn't checked in). The walk item by item gave the same line, and so did 0.0.9 up to the width where the space before the control fits the line before it.
- Chrome and Safari give a chip, an inline-block as wide as its text, its text's width rounded up to their layout unit, where rich inline adds Canvas's width: in webkit-host `#42` in 700 12px Helvetica Neue with 6px of padding on each side is 32.03125px wide, where Canvas gives 32.016px, and `@bob` 43.609375px against 43.596px (Chrome: 43.6016px), 2026-09-30. A line of several chips can then wrap where rich inline fits it, as the roundings add up past the WebKit profile's 1/64px fit allowance: `@maya`, a 20px box, `@bob`, ` and `, `#42`, ` `, a 20px box, ` ` and `done`, the chips as above, take 2 lines in webkit-host at 253.23px where rich inline fits them on 1 (`bun harness explain` of a review probe's case, not a harness case).
- Rare: an atomic item whose own text holds a line or paragraph separator, which no draw of the real-usage sample holds, in the WebKit profile, where the separator ends a line. The item is as wide as its text's first line, since a chip's width is that of its text laid out as one line of its own (`prepareRichInline()`), and its text leaves the separator out: on the unit tests' font, where a letter is 9.6px, a chip `a`, U+2028, `b` is 9.6px wide with the text `ab` (2026-10-10). What Safari does with such an inline-block wasn't recorded.
- `extraWidth` is charged on every piece of an item split across lines, where browsers pad only the first piece's start and the last one's end (a `600 13px Menlo` item with 12px of it takes a line more than Firefox at 46-48px; 2026-09-14). In pre-wrap, Safari hangs the spaces that start the next item on the line of a padded span's last piece, which only its end edge pads, where rich inline's piece overflows and the spaces start the next line (2 webkit-host cases of the rich set, `line-feed-starts-padded-item` at 44 and 47.4px), and Chrome, padding only the first piece's start, fits `Unbreakable   ` and a 20px-padded `  spaced code` at 81px in a line fewer (1 Chrome case of the rich set). Padding per piece is on the API discussion's list (TODO.md).
- An item whose text is empty takes no room, `extraWidth` included, and outside pre-wrap an item of only white space leaves its collapsed space and no `extraWidth`, where all three browsers give a padded span its padding either way: in 16px Arial, `left`, an empty span with 11px of padding on each side and `right` are 74.47px wide where rich inline gives 52.47px, and with a space in the span 78.91px against 56.91px, the widths without the padding. In the Gecko profile an item of white space and bidi controls is such an item, as the analysis joins the controls with the space: `ab`, a 20px item of a space and U+202C with 4px of padding on each side, and `cd` in 16px Arial are 48.25px wide in Firefox, two lines up to 48px, and 40.25px in rich inline, a line short from 41 to 48px (8 of 17 layouts at 38 to 52px, 60 and 100px; Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-07, a probe that isn't checked in). An atomic item of only white space keeps its padding, as an object as wide as its `extraWidth`, as an inline-block of only white space is laid out. In pre-wrap the item of a space keeps its `extraWidth`, and the empty one still loses it (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, Firefox 156.0.1 and webkit-host, 2026-10-01). Dropping an empty item is kept, since apps hide runs with it, and a box is the item for room without text (RESEARCH.md, Objects Inside A Line).
- A padded item that starts with white space right before a line or paragraph separator: Safari keeps the item's start edge and that space on the line before it, however far they overflow, where the WebKit profile counts the space after the item's start edge and gives the two a line of their own where they don't fit. Of `ab` and an item of a space, U+2028 and `cd` with 4px of padding on each side in 16px Arial, Safari has 2 lines from 26px (`ab`, then `cd`), 3 at 18 to 24px and 4 at 12 and 16px, and rich inline a line more at each of the six widths tried under 26px, the edge and the space alone on it, 12.45px wide (6 of 13 layouts at 12 to 100px; webkit-host, WebKit 22625.1.29.11.27, 2026-10-07, a probe that isn't checked in; not traced in WebKit's source). The layouts are the same with a CR ending the item before (White space and controls, the lone CR's entry).
- In a box narrower than the letter before a space, Safari gives a U+2028 after that space a line of its own, with nothing visible, where the text walkers hang the space and end that letter's line at the separator, as at a hard break in one text: `abc`, a space and U+2028 before an item of one space have that fourth line up to 7.97px and not from 7.98px in 16px Arial, whose `c` is 8px wide, and so does a separator that starts an item after `hello` and a space (3 webkit-host cases of the rich set at 1 to 9px, on its accepted list; the 7.97px is from the width search of 2026-10-07 in webkit-host). Not traced in WebKit's source.
- `line-height: 1` on a fragment's element, the README's way to keep text of another size inside the line, stops holding as that text's size nears the line height: in a 16px/20px Arial paragraph, Arial up to 18px leaves the line 20px tall, at 19px it makes the line about 0.5px taller in all three browsers, and 18px Verdana does by 0.5-0.75px; 18px text in 14px/20px Arial adds 0.25-0.5px, and 19 and 20px text in 16px/24px Arial nothing (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, Firefox 156.0.1 and webkit-host, 2026-10-01). The README doesn't give the limit.
- Safari fits a padded span's first piece, its text up to its first break, with the span's start edge alone, in both white-space modes, where the WebKit profile fits the whole `extraWidth`, as Firefox does. WebKit places a line's content one run between soft wrap opportunities at a time (`nextWrapOpportunity`, `InlineFormattingUtils.cpp:456-545`, from `InlineLineBuilder.cpp:543`) and keeps a run that fits whole: the cloned end edges it takes off the line's room first are those of boxes already on the line, not of a box the run itself opens, whose start edge is in the run's width (`handleInlineContentWithClonedDecoration`, `InlineLineBuilder.cpp:1583-1586`; `placedClonedDecorationWidth`, `:1501-1523`; WebKit 7625.1.29.11.27). It counts that box's end edge only where it breaks the run inside (`clonedDecorationAtBreakingPosition`, `:1525-1559`, `:1598-1603`) and for every later run, on that line, where the box is now on the line's list (`Line::appendInlineBoxStart`, `InlineLine.cpp:313-314`), and on later ones, which start with it (`Line::initialize`, `InlineLine.cpp:60-77`). The line's end adds the edge whatever fit (`Line::close`, `InlineLine.cpp:89-102`), so that line can be wider than its paragraph by up to the end edge, which is read from the source and not measured. So from the width where the line's earlier text, the span's start edge and its first piece fit, for one end edge more, Safari keeps the piece and rich inline moves the span to the next line, or, where the span starts the line, breaks inside the piece. `Use `, a 14px Menlo span `system-ui` with 4px of padding on each side and ` here` in 16px Helvetica Neue are `Use system-`, 95.59px wide, and `ui here` at 96-99.5px in webkit-host, and `Use`, `system-ui`, `here` in rich inline; that span starting the paragraph keeps `system-` at 63-66.5px, where rich inline's lines are `system`, `-ui`, `here`. Of 2,633 layouts of 19 paragraphs in normal white space (seven font pairs, 4-20px of padding, a first piece that ends at a hyphen, a space or between CJK characters, the span after text, a chip, a box or another padded span, starting the paragraph or a later line, and in a right-to-left paragraph), webkit-host fails 229, 189 with a wrong line count and 40 with a character on another line, in ranges as wide as the span's end padding, and none of their 2,633 twins without padding; Firefox 156.0.1 passes all of them, as Gecko takes the end edge off every line's room (`nsInlineFrame.cpp:518-519`), and Chrome 154.0.8037.57 fails 466, on the span's later pieces too (Pre-wrap, above). Under pre-wrap 12 of 202 fail, in the same ranges. A span of one piece, whose run closes the box (all 101 layouts), one that starts with a collapsible space, which opens the box in a run of its own (all 57), and the span's later pieces on its first line pass (`Run `, such a span `git log --oneline` and ` now` at 92-106px: all 57, where Chrome fails 16). The rich set doesn't show it, since none of the widths it pins for webkit-host is in such a range: at every 0.5px, webkit-host fails 118 of 7,389 layouts of the set's nine templates with a padded span in normal white space, all of them such ranges (`Use `, `prepareWithSegments(text, font)` at 192.5-199 and 223-229.5px; a lone `git commit --amend --no-edit` at 29.5-36px; `Run `, `bun run check` at 29.5-36 and 60.5-67px; and #120's `inline code`, with 6px, at 50.5-56, 65.5-71, 95.5-101 and 185.5-191px, 48 of that sentence's 701 layouts). Recorded again in reversed order, each case in a fresh document, every one of these layouts came out the same (webkit-host on WebKit 22625.1.29.11.27 at device pixel ratio 2, 2026-10-04; installed Safari wasn't run, and padding that differs by side wasn't tried). A port fits an item's first piece, up to its first break and joined to the text before it where no break comes between, with half its `extraWidth` in the WebKit profile, as `paddedOpeningFit`'s `'placed'` does for an opening of white space or a hard break (`src/measurement.ts`). On the paragraph's handle that is a line-end trim on the item's segments up to its first break, as the start edge of an opening has (`lineEndTrims`, `getOpeningFit()` in `src/rich-inline.ts`), with no second place to keep it in. `extraWidth` isn't split by side, so half of it is the premise a tab's offset already takes (Pre-wrap, above). The line such a port reports is wider than its width by up to the end edge, as Safari's is by the source, so measuring that line in Safari comes first. As one profile value the fit would cover Chrome's gap too: Chrome reserves the end edge on no piece, WebKit on all but the run that opens the span, Gecko on all. Cases would come from a short rich-set template with a padded span of two pieces, such as `Use `, `system-ui`, ` here`, recorded in all three browsers.
- Firefox pads a span with `box-decoration-break: clone` once for each bidi level run of its text on a line, where rich inline counts an item's `extraWidth` once on a line, as Chrome and Safari pad. Bidi resolution makes a text frame of each level run and splits the inline frames around it there (`nsBidiPresUtils.cpp:1039-1053`; `CreateContinuation` and `SplitInlineAncestors`, `:704-758`, `:612-660`), unless the run ends with the span's last child (`:1115-1147`); under clone every continuation takes its start and end edges (`nsInlineFrame::ReflowFrames`, `nsInlineFrame.cpp:504-513`, `:654-674`) and the line fits each as a frame (`CanPlaceFrame`, `nsLineLayout.cpp:1189-1270`), and the reordering that moves `slice` edges to the first and last continuation leaves clone's alone (`RepositionFrame`, `nsBidiPresUtils.cpp:1800-1826`; Firefox 156.0). Blink keeps one box of a span on a line, split only where reordering pulls its pieces apart, with one start and one end edge among them (`UpdateBoxDataFragmentRange`, `UpdateFragmentEdges`, `inline_box_state.cc:726-843`, Chromium 153), and WebKit gives a box's sides to its first and last display box on the line (`adjustVisualGeometryForDisplayBox`, `InlineDisplayContentBuilder.cpp:768-796`). So a padded span of two level runs is one `extraWidth` wider in Firefox than in rich inline, and one of three is two wider, in a paragraph of either direction: code that starts or ends with characters that take the paragraph's direction, beside right-to-left text or the paragraph's edge (`--a`, `/usr/bin`, `foo()` in a right-to-left paragraph, one with no right-to-left character too, since `direction: rtl` alone turns the resolution on); text of both directions (`abc אבג` is two boxes, `ab ثم cd` three); and a bidi control in the span (`a`, RLM, `b` is three boxes, and `ab`, RLM two, the second only padding). One level run is one box: `abc`, `--`, `12`, `ab--cd`, `git commit --amend`, and `--a` between Latin words. `وليس `, a 13px Menlo span `--a` with 3px of padding on each side and ` ثم بعد` in pre-wrap 15px Helvetica Neue, right-to-left, at 45px keep `--a` alone on the second line in Firefox, 35.5px in two boxes, where rich inline has 29.5px and fits ` ثم` after it: the right line count with a word on another line. Of 851 layouts of seven such paragraphs (3-7px of padding, four font pairs, 28-260px in whole pixels, normal and pre-wrap) 305 fail in Firefox 156.0.1, 91 with a wrong line count and 214 with a word on another line, each width where a line starts to fit being one `extraWidth` higher than predicted; all 788 of their twins with one level run in the span pass (`abc` for `--a`), as do all 1,639 of both without padding (2026-10-04). Over five font pairs, Hebrew and Arabic context and 1, 7 and 20px of padding, all 60 layouts of a two-run span are two boxes with four paddings of edges in Firefox, and one box with two in Chrome and webkit-host. Chrome 154.0.8037.57 and webkit-host fail 92 and 18 of the 851 and 67 and 12 of the twins, each at a line that ends inside the span, which is their fit of a span's end edge (above, and Pre-wrap) and not this. With `dir="ltr"` on the span Firefox makes one box of left-to-right code and lays out all 385 widths of three of the paragraphs as rich inline has them, where it has 217 without; `dir="auto"` and `unicode-bidi: plaintext` give one box too, `unicode-bidi: isolate` or `embed` alone doesn't, and a span of both directions stays split. No harness case holds the shape: none of the rich set's 954 right-to-left cases or the sample's 628 has a padded span, and in the one padded case whose paragraph holds a right-to-left character, a left-to-right one of the sample, the padding is an atomic chip's, `@田中` before Hebrew text, and an inline-block doesn't split. A port needs the paragraph's direction and Firefox's levels over the items' joined text, neither of which Pretext takes (Bidi levels, direction and script runs; the port of the levels at 8e88756b took every paragraph as left-to-right), and then, in the Gecko profile, an `extraWidth` for each level run of an item on a line, each fit as a frame of its own. Short of that, a `dir` on the span closes the commonest shape, code in a right-to-left paragraph, from the app's side; whether the README asks for it is open. Margins under clone weren't measured.
- Chrome's and Safari's spans reserve a hyphen's width for a soft hyphen right before a ZWSP or a space, at some widths; neither walker does (2026-09-12).
- In a seeded search, 7-14 rich-inline paragraphs per profile still take more lines as the width grows (#327, 2026-09-15; not rerun since #359, #369 and the one-paragraph design changed where rich lines end).
- A line that holds only an atomic item of white space, an object as wide as its `extraWidth`, has no text for the harness's recorder to list, so the scorer takes a prediction with that line for a line too many where the recorded height has the line: of 1,276 layouts of such chips in two probes, the paragraph's line count is the recorded height's in every one in all three browsers, main's is not in 188, 187 and 188 of them in Chrome, Firefox and webkit-host, and 94, 74 and 94 that main passes are scored as failing (2026-10-05; harness/README.md, what the harness doesn't see).
- The Gecko scan reads a paragraph's joined text as 8-bit or 16-bit as a whole, where Firefox's text runs are one or the other each: a paragraph with one character above U+00FF takes the 16-bit rules for all its items (`getGeckoLineBreaks()`'s `is8bit`; not probed).

### Language and generic families

- Preparation reads one language, `setLocale()`'s or else `<html lang>`, never an element's `lang` or a `Content-Language`, and no canvas follows either; this waits for the API discussion at the end of the project (TODO.md; RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-09-26). It costs Chrome's `line_normal_cj.brk`, which Chrome opens for `zh` content and, under a Chinese UI, for content without a language (`<html lang=en><p lang="">中文””tail` breaks before `tail` in Chrome only; 22 cases, on no list, since the harness pins Chrome's UI to en-US), and Safari's Japanese table for a `lang="ja"` element on an `en` page (`||||““tail` doesn't break before the quotes, where the WebKit scan under `en` does; 34 cases; 2 / 0 / 0; 2026-09-23).
- Safari's generic families by language, still open (2026-09-24): fallback for characters a named family lacks and `system-ui`'s fallback order, which follow the page language on the page and the system's in Canvas; a Han page whose user's first Chinese language is a Traditional one; iOS's `monospace` under `fa`, `ug`, `ps`, `sd` and Arabic with a region, and `fantasy` under `he` and `yi`, left at macOS's answers; and iOS 27's own answers, never extracted (the table's iOS answers come from the iOS 26 simulator). WebKit #285993, a canvas `lang` that defaults to the document's, would settle them all.
- Safari's `ui-sans-serif`, `ui-serif`, `ui-rounded` and `ui-monospace` show the same gap as `system-ui` (above). On this Mac, whose first language is `zh-CN`, a Chinese and a Japanese paragraph under `<html lang="ja">` break otherwise than Pretext at 53 and 46 of 56 layouts each in `system-ui`, `ui-sans-serif`, `ui-rounded` and `ui-monospace`, and at none in `ui-serif`; an Arabic one under `lang="ur"` at all 56 in `system-ui`, `ui-sans-serif`, `ui-serif` and `ui-rounded`, and at none in `ui-monospace`; and none of them under `lang="en"` or `lang="zh-Hant"` (webkit-host, 2026-10-01). Safari 26.0.1 in the iOS 26.0 simulator has the same four wrong under `ja`, at 51 and 42 of 56. The README names only `system-ui` and `-apple-system`, and only macOS, and leaves the rest to its sentence on generic fonts.
- The WebKit scan's use of Safari's `Intl.Segmenter` words in Thai, Lao, Khmer and Myanmar runs isn't checked against Safari's dictionary breaks, as Chrome's and Firefox's are; the rebuild's WebKit port, which reads the same words, differs from libicucore's line iterator at 27 of 282,337 positions, all where a range starts with a combining mark (RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data).
- Safari's line rules for ICU's alias locales and three-letter language tags: the WebKit profile's lookup (`getWebKitLineRules()`, `src/line-breaks.ts`) cuts a tag back subtag by subtag, so it reads `zh-TW` as `zh` where libicucore resolves it to `zh_Hant`, with another quotation remap, and `jpn` and `kor` as the root's table where ICU opens `ja`'s and `ko`'s. Against `ubrk_open(UBRK_LINE)` in macOS 27's libicucore on 20,000 random strings, the rule boundaries differ on 185 for `zh-TW`, `zh-HK`, `zh-MO` and `uz-AF`, 386 for `sh`, 601 for `iw`, 985 for `mo`, 1,617 for `kor` and 2,189 for `jpn`, and on none for `zh-Hant-TW`, `he`, `ja` or any of the 31 tags `harness/icu.test.ts` checks, which leaves these out (2026-09-30). How many of Safari's breaks that moves isn't measured: its scan settles most quotation marks before it asks ICU.

### Fitting arithmetic

Each engine's line fit is exact in its own units (RESEARCH.md, Measurement Model); the Blink and Gecko profiles fit with a 0.005px allowance instead, a named gap, not a tolerance to tune. Its cost in the harness sits under the "Within 1/64 px of where the browser's lines change" headings, most on Chrome's list; on a rich line of several text items it reaches whole-pixel widths too (below). Chrome's real-usage Japanese lines, which fit one more character than the Canvas widths add up to, aren't that cost: they miss by 1-4px, the kerning between kana that Chrome's Canvas doesn't report (Kerning Chrome's Canvas doesn't report). The same kerning moves keep-all groups: in 16px Hiragino Sans Chrome lays `日本語のテキストです。` out 173.45px wide, where Canvas measures 174.40px, holding only the kerning of `す。`, and its graphemes add up to 176px, so it fits that line at 173.6px and `日本語のテキストで` in 143.05px, which the Chromium profile, summing standalone graphemes where a keep-all group breaks, doesn't, in one text node as in spans; Firefox and Safari fit them as Pretext does (5 Chrome cases of the rich set's keep-all templates, 2026-09-29). Chrome fits on a grid of 1/64 zoomed px, and the zoom includes the device pixel ratio: `14.1px "Helvetica Neue"` at 112px kept `over the lazy dog.` on one line at DPR 1 though Canvas measured 112.0103px, and broke before `dog.` at DPR 2, as Pretext does (Chrome 153, 2026-09-14). Reading the ratio is an API question (#321's decision 4; RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends; TODO.md).
- Chrome rounds up once for each inline item, not once for a line, so a line of several text items is wider in Chrome than its Canvas widths add up to. Every text node is an item at least, a styled span's, an unstyled span's and one text node beside another alike (`AppendItem`, `inline_items_builder.cc:980`; text shaped across items is split back per item, `inline_node.cc:1743-1781`), and each item's piece of a line is rounded up to the unit before the line's position adds it (`SnappedWidth`, `shape_result_view.h:124`, over `LayoutUnit::FromFloatCeil`, `layout_unit.h:134-136`; `inline_size` and `position_`, `line_breaker.cc:1714`, `:1420`; Chromium 153). The unit is 1/64 of a zoomed px, 1/128px at DPR 2, so a line of n items is up to n units wider than its Canvas sum, and rich inline, which adds the Canvas widths unrounded, keeps a word Chrome wraps, the other way from the allowance's own misses. Items `Is it `, italic `really`, ` `, 700 `that` and ` simple? Mostly: the hard part` in 16px Helvetica Neue are 313.872px by Canvas and 313.891px on Chrome's line at DPR 2 (3,793 + 4,703 + 570 + 3,832 + 27,280 units, each item's Canvas width rounded up), so Chrome wraps `part` under 313.883px and rich inline keeps it down to 313.867px: at 313.875px Chrome lays out 2 lines and rich inline 1 (`bun harness explain` of a README check's case, not a harness case; Firefox wraps it there too, as the Gecko profile does, and webkit-host keeps it, as the WebKit profile does; how those two add items up wasn't traced). The items' rounded widths against the truncated width plus one unit gave Chrome's line count on all 16,800 layouts of 240 lines of 7-9 words in six fonts, cut seven ways into 1 to 7 items as styled spans, unstyled spans and bare text nodes, at 10 widths within 3 units of the items' sum at DPR 2, and on all 14,400 of six of the cuts at DPR 1. Five items came out 0.005-0.034px wider than Pretext's sum at DPR 2 and 0.012-0.068px at DPR 1, seven up to 0.045px and 0.086px, and none wider in 16px Arial at DPR 2, whose advances are whole units there. It reaches whole-pixel widths: of 4,816 layouts of 16 Gatsby paragraphs with every fourth word italic or bold at each whole width from 200 to 500px, rich inline kept a word Chrome wraps in 10 in 16px Helvetica Neue, 13 in 15px Georgia, 2 of them wrong line counts, and none in 16px Arial at DPR 2, and in 16, 19 and 2 at DPR 1, 1, 2 and 1 of them wrong line counts, each a line that fits rounded once and not rounded per item; with every ninth word styled, 1 in Helvetica Neue and 9 in Georgia at DPR 2. The same paragraphs in one text node have none that way, only the allowance's misses the other way, 4 to 8 layouts a font at DPR 2 (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04). Plain text that an app paints as several text nodes is rounded per node the same way, which `prepare()` can't know. A text node that holds several bidi level runs is as many items, which Blink splits it into (`SegmentBidiRuns`, `inline_node.cc:1333-1436`, Chromium 153), so plain text that mixes right-to-left words with numbers or Latin words is rounded once a run, in one node too: `כן 3 של 2 של 1 כן` in `15px "Helvetica Neue", "Arial Hebrew"` is 105.990px by Canvas and 106.016px on Chrome's line, 3.3 units over, so Chrome wraps its last word in a 106px box and `prepare()` keeps one line, where seven Hebrew words alone, one run, are 135.2848px by Canvas and 135.2891px on Chrome's line, the one rounding (`bun harness explain`, Chrome 154.0.8037.98 at DPR 2, 2026-10-10; not a harness case, and the real-usage sample wasn't searched for the shape). A port rounds each fragment up to the unit where rich inline adds it to the line and fits against the truncated width plus one unit, in place of the allowance. That needs the ratio (above) and, at a ratio other than 1, widths from a Canvas at the zoomed size: 12 of 7,440 items at DPR 2 were a unit off as rounded from Canvas at the CSS size. Cases would be rich lines of 3, 5 and 7 text items in a font whose advances aren't whole units, a unit either side of the items' rounded sum and at a whole-pixel width; none was added. Windows and Android weren't run.

### Contexts kept for the page's life

Main keeps one measurement context, replaced only when preparation's language changes, so a context that goes stale stays stale: Firefox 156's, first used before Firefox reads its late family names (PLATFORM_BUGS.md, the late family names), and WebKit's that missed a `FontFace` loaded before joining an empty `document.fonts` (PLATFORM_BUGS.md; `clearCache()` and a second prepare don't heal it on main, and preparing another font before the `clearCache()` does, 2026-10-05). The per-engine rebuild makes a context per paragraph in Firefox; those run at 0.92 of a kept context's Firefox speed on the bench's chat messages and 0.75-0.80 on plain ASCII (2026-09-20); main has neither taken nor rejected them.

### Emoji correction

The correction is one width per font, read once from a DOM span and kept until `clearCache()` or a change of language, but the gap it corrects depends on the device pixel ratio times the page zoom: for a 16px Arial emoji, Chrome's Canvas says 20px at every ratio and its page 20, 17.6, 16 and 16px at ratios 1, 1.25, 1.5 and 2, and Firefox's Canvas 21px and its page 21px at ratio 1 and 16px at 2. So once the ratio crosses from 1.25 or under to 1.5 or over, or back, as a page zoomed past 125% on a 1x display does (a window dragged between a 1x and a Retina display is inferred to; both displays here are Retina), every handle, and every later prepare in a font already read, is off by 4-5px an emoji until `clearCache()` and a new prepare. That crossing is the 16px one: at 12px Chrome's gap is none at ratios 1 and 1.25, 1px at 1.5, 1.9px at 1.75 and 3px from 2, and at 10 and 11px it still grows between ratios 2 and 3 (2 and 2.5px against 3 and 3px in Chrome, 1.5 and 3px against 3 and 4px in Firefox); from 12 to 32px it is the same within 0.5px at 2, 2.2, 2.5 and 3 in both, each read in a browser started at that ratio (2026-10-01). So the README asks for `clearCache()` and a new prepare after any change of the ratio (#418). Over the sample's 294 plain in-claims cases with an emoji, nearly all with one, prepared at ratio 2 and laid out at 1: 6 break wrongly in Chrome and 6 in Firefox where none did (about 2% of their weight), 1 with a wrong line count, and about three quarters of bubbles sized to the widest line are 4-5px too narrow for it, which the browser then wraps; prepared at 1 and laid out at 2, they are that much too wide. With three emoji in the emoji's place, 6-8 have a wrong height and 17-22 a wrong break (Chrome 154.0.8037.57 with its zoom changed under a live page, Firefox 156.0.1 at `layout.css.devPixelsPerPx` 1 against 2, 2026-09-30). Read at the ratio it's used at, the correction leaves nothing at ratios 1, 2 and 3 and up to 0.6px an emoji at fractional ones. A fix reads the ratio when preparing, a new hidden input and so the maintainer's call (RESEARCH.md, Limits), and must drop the cached fit advances too, which hold the old correction (#321's side finding 6 inferred this, unmeasured). In a worker it stays 0 (PLATFORM_BUGS.md; #292), and a hand-off from the page is on the API discussion's list (TODO.md). In Firefox 156.0.1 it can also be read as 0: for the first 0.2 s of two probes whose texts hold combining marks of other scripts, U+1F600 itself measured 17px, Firefox's missing-glyph box (below), in Canvas and in the span alike, so 19 of the 20 fonts first prepared then took no correction, all but the one that names the emoji font (12 and 14px, in most runs, 2026-10-01). Measuring U+1F600 on the page and in Canvas before each font's first `prepare()` made it go away.

Which glyphs take it is read off Canvas widths (`countEmojiGlyphs` in `src/measurement.ts`): in a grapheme that holds an emoji or a pictograph, each stretch of emoji characters (emoji and pictographs, ZWJ, skin tones, tags, U+20E3 and the variation selectors) is measured whole, apart from the rest of the grapheme, and takes one correction for each emoji width it measures, none where it measures anything else. That rests on the emoji font giving every glyph one advance, as Apple Color Emoji does, and only macOS was measured: where Canvas has the gap and the emoji font's advances vary, nothing would be corrected, where one correction per grapheme, the rule before, corrected each. A correction other than 0 read on another platform would reopen it. The gaps (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, Firefox 156.0.1, DPR 2, 2026-10-01):
- A glyph of another font exactly as wide as an emoji's Canvas width takes the correction. Two turned up, among 652 emoji graphemes in the 258 installed families and 11 generic ones at 13 and 16px, 214 pictographs and keycaps with no U+FE0F in the same families at ten sizes from 11 to 22px, and the 5,225 forms of emoji-test.txt 17.0 in 42 fonts:
  - Zapfino's `™` is 1.25em wide, which is Chrome's Canvas width of an emoji at 12 and 16px.
  - Firefox's box for a character no font has is the wider of 17px, for a code point past U+FFFF, and the font's average character width (`gfxShapedText::SetMissingGlyph`, `gfxFont.cpp:859-863`, over `gfxFontMissingGlyphs::GetDesiredMinWidth`, `gfxFontMissingGlyphs.cpp:530-544`): 17px wherever the font's average character is narrower than that, as in ordinary text fonts at the sizes that take a correction, and 17px is its Canvas width of an emoji at 13px. At 13px the box measures 4px narrow: for a pictograph code point no font has (U+1F02C, not assigned, in 263 of the 269 families), and for about a second after a page first uses a pictograph that Firefox finds only by searching every installed family, such as U+1F336 with no U+FE0F, which it draws as the box while the families' character maps load (`gfxPlatformFontList::GlobalFontFallback`, `gfxPlatformFontList.cpp:1480-1485`; 1.5 s later it was the emoji font's glyph). A width measured meanwhile stays cached, at any size, as it did before the glyph count.
  - A keycap with no U+FE0F (`1`, U+20E3) isn't asked for that reason: `1` and Chrome's missing glyph for U+20E3 add up to 1.25em in Baskerville and Palatino. Where the font list names the emoji font, which then draws that keycap, it measures 4px wide in Chrome at 14px, as it did before.
- A stretch that two fonts draw takes no correction, though the emoji font's glyphs in it carry the gap. One correction per grapheme, the rule before, was right there wherever the grapheme held one such glyph. All but one of the shapes found are ones only fuzzing produces, so they are named here and not modelled (RESEARCH.md, Limits; Decisions Log, 2026-10-01):
  - The one real text can hold, in Firefox: a ZWJ sequence written with no U+FE0F after a first character whose presentation is text by default. Firefox matches a font character by character (`gfxFontGroup::FindFontForChar`, `gfxTextRun.cpp:3178-3194`), draws that character from a text font and the rest from the emoji font, and the text measures 5px wide. Three forms of emoji-test.txt 17.0 do, each the unqualified form of its sequence: `2764 200D 1F525`, `2764 200D 1FA79` and `26D3 200D 1F4A5` (heart on fire, mending heart, broken chain), each in 36 or 37 of 42 fonts. Chrome draws them as one glyph of the emoji font and measures them right. Of the 20,412 widths of emoji-test.txt's unqualified forms, alone and inside a word, 18,985 are right in Firefox, against 10,724 before the glyph count, and 216 of those three were right and are wrong.
  - Made up: a character a text font draws (`©`, Menlo's `⚡`, U+231A before U+FE0E) joined by a ZWJ to an emoji or a sequence, or before a skin tone; in Chrome, an emoji joined by a ZWJ to a skin tone (U+1F431, ZWJ, U+1F3FD); and a keycap mark that the emoji font draws after such a character (U+26A1, U+FE0E, U+20E3 in 16px Zapfino in Chrome is 25.98px on the page and 29.98px predicted).
  - For about a second after a Firefox page first uses a pictograph whose presentation is text by default, which it draws as its missing-glyph box (above), that box before a skin tone: U+1F590 and a skin tone, in the first five fonts a probe measured.
  - Over 496 graphemes that mix fonts and 614 that mix them in more than one place, in 90 fonts, 44,640 and 55,260 widths: 4,112 and 15,812 in Chrome and 7,847 and 25,670 in Firefox were wrong under one correction per grapheme and are right; 178 and 2,338 in Chrome and 252 and 3,070 in Firefox were right and are wrong. Firefox's counts are over 71 of the fonts: in that run 19 of the first 20 took no correction with any build (above).
  - Asking such a stretch character by character, each with its variation selector, and bounding the count by the emoji widths that fit in the grapheme was measured beside this and left out (branch `emoji-correction`, unmerged): those last counts were 0 and 602 in Chrome and 0 and 166 in Firefox, and Firefox's three unqualified forms were right, with every harness prediction and every line count of the two realistic probe sets the same, for 13 more runtime lines, a second pattern, and a rule for which characters to ask alone that is neither engine's (the PR, #398, has both tables). Such sequences turning up in real text would reopen it.
- A stretch measured apart has lost its context: Chrome draws a skin tone in the cluster of a combining mark before it as the named font's missing glyph (`HarfBuzzShaper::ExtractShapeResults`, `harfbuzz_shaper.cc:586-655`), where the tone measured alone is an emoji and takes a correction. 1,100 of Chrome's 2,338 above are an emoji sequence, such a mark, then a skin tone.
- In Chrome, a skin tone right after a letter, a digit, a kana or an ideograph is the first font's missing glyph, as wide as U+0378 there, on the page and in Canvas alike: Chrome sends a cluster to the next font whole (`HarfBuzzShaper::ExtractShapeResults`, `harfbuzz_shaper.cc:586-655`), and no font has both. The library measures the tone apart from the characters around it, as an emoji: `ab`, U+1F3FB, `cd` at 16px is 46.70px on the page and as one text in Canvas and 50.70px predicted in Arial, 44.16px and 52.16px in Helvetica Neue (before the glyph count too; 807 of the 265,140 widths in 30 font lists). Georgia's and Verdana's missing glyph is as wide as the page's emoji, 16px, so they come out right. Firefox draws these as Canvas measures them apart, less the correction. A line can break before the tone, so it is a segment of its own, and measuring each grapheme whole, the form a rule for Chrome's clusters would take, leaves the same 807 wrong; over the 44,640 widths of graphemes that mix fonts, 5,728 that were right became wrong with it in Chrome, against 178, since Chrome ends the emoji font's run before a mark of another script.
- With `"Apple Color Emoji"` first in the font list, the emoji font draws the spaces too, and they carry the gap uncounted: `x`, space, U+231A, space, `y` at 11px is 45.5px on the page and 50.5px predicted in Chrome (before the glyph count too).

### Canvas answers that differ from the page

Unfiled; PLATFORM_BUGS.md takes any that gets filed.
- Safari's Canvas gives isolated and fallback-font combining marks an advance they don't have in context (by 2026-09-12, Safari 26.5.2).
- Safari's OffscreenCanvas gives a space before U+FE0F the emoji's width, 21px at 16px, where the page draws a space, 4.45px (2026-09-15, Safari 26.5.2).
- Chrome's Canvas gives VS16 about 4.9px that the page doesn't (by 2026-09-12).
- Firefox's Canvas puts a text-final ZWJ in a left-to-right bidi run of its own: `آگ`, ZWJ measures 16.32px in 16px Arial, where the page joins them at 9.97px (2026-09-23).
- Firefox's OffscreenCanvas measures synthetic bold wider than its page lays it out, at any device pixel ratio above 1. For a face drawn bold without a bold face of its family, Gecko adds an offset to every cluster's advance: 0.25 + 0.75 × S / 48 px at a size S under 48 px and S / 48 px from there (`GetSyntheticBoldOffset`, `gfxFont.h:1899-1904`; `PostShapingFixup`, `gfxFont.cpp:3551-3562`), rounded to an app unit, a sixtieth of a CSS px (`ApplyTrackingToClusters`, `gfxFont.cpp:901-905`). S is the size the text is shaped at, the CSS size for OffscreenCanvas and the device size for the page (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Firefox: OffscreenCanvas against the DOM), so at ratio r Canvas is 0.25 × (1 − 1/r) px a cluster wider than the page while S × r is under 48, 0.125 px at ratio 2, and the difference then shrinks to nothing at 48 px. Measured on the 22 characters of `Create a Query Letter:` at weight 700 at ratio 2, page against Canvas: 16px Roboto 162.33 and 164.95 px, 16px Inter 176.20 and 178.77, 14px Roboto 142.32 and 144.88, 10px Roboto 102.38 and 105.00, each 7 app units a cluster after rounding, 24px Roboto 241.77 and 244.70 (8 units), 30px Roboto 302.47 and 304.35 (5), and 48px Roboto 483.72 in both; at weight 400 the two agree, as they do in Arial, which has a bold face, and at ratio 1 (`--scale=1`) in all six sizes of Roboto and Inter tried. A fixed-pitch face is widened only at its spaces, which take the offset through the font's space width (`gfxMacFont.cpp:389-391`) where `PostShapingFixup` skips a face whose widest advance is its average: 16px Monaco is 212.35 and 212.70 px, the three spaces' 21 units (Firefox 156.0.1, 2026-10-05). So a line that holds synthetic bold is charged too much and can end a word early, in any proportional family a page bolds without serving or installing its bold face. The harness serves Roboto and Inter in Regular only: the two real-usage cases on Firefox's list, a Roboto paragraph with a bold span each, pass with the span at weight 400. Chrome's and Safari's Canvas agree with their pages on synthetic bold (16px and 30px Roboto and 16px Inter, the same phrase; Chrome 154.0.8037.57 and webkit-host, 2026-10-05). The per-engine rebuild has this as one of the two named costs of measuring on OffscreenCanvas alone, beside the width one app unit off, and met it as a symbol from a fallback font in a bold span, 7 app units a glyph at 16px and 8 at 24px as here (`rebuild/specs/gecko-RESULTS.md`). Its unmerged `r4-gecko-alt-synthetic-bold` tells synthetic bold from Canvas by measuring at two sizes, which the offset isn't proportional to, for emoji clusters only (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, DOM And Canvas-Element Paths): a fix for words starts there.

### Cost

- A run of combining marks after an invisible format character, such as a word joiner, or a control prepares in linear time since #351, but each run's context still holds the grapheme before the chain: `x` with 4,000 U+0301, then 4,000 pairs of a control and a run of marks, no two runs alike, a 20,001-unit text, submits 33 million units to Canvas offline in about 8,000 calls, over a second on a fake Canvas (64 million units before #351, 2026-09-26). With the same mark in every pair, the width cache answers all but about 100 of those calls, and 0.4 million units are submitted (rechecked at 8e88756b, 2026-09-30).
- A word cut between letters costs text not measured before more Canvas calls since #435: the Blink profile's questions about letter pairs, 7.2% more calls on the real-usage sample and 10.1% on interface labels, and the Gecko profile's question about ligatures, 3.1% on the sample; the WebKit profile asks nothing more. The Gecko profile's prefixes themselves, one call per letter of a word of 80px or wider since #340, cost new text more: 760 of the 1,557 calls German's 200 interface labels take, where the ligature questions are 64, and Firefox prepares new labels of seven languages 17-36% slower than 0.0.9 since the two changes (RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data; Firefox 156.0.1, the calls counted on 2026-10-05 and the labels timed on main as of #461, 2026-10-09). Emergency breaks inside a word has the counts by language, how they fall as a font sees text, and the cheaper forms that were measured and not taken. `layout()` of a text whose long words are cut reads slower too. The bench's long breakable runs, with hundreds of words cut at each width, read 7.9% and 9.7% slower in Chrome 154 in two foreground runs, for one number read at each line that starts inside a word, and 8.2% and 13.9% slower in Firefox 156.0.1, where the work is the same as before and one whole-number advance among fractional ones leaves SpiderMonkey's compiled loop in its slower state more often. The zero written as -0, or the advances in typed arrays, would remove Firefox's, and neither is taken (RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, JavaScript Engines; Decisions Log, 2026-10-05; two foreground bench runs of three sessions, 2026-10-05).
- The kerning with spaces reads a character's scripts for each character the search back from a space passes, and for every character since the last read where that search meets a closing bracket or a character of several scripts (`readScriptRuns()` in `src/prepare.ts`): one table lookup each since #423, where a character outside ASCII took up to five tests of Unicode classes. No bench row has Cyrillic or Greek text in a font that kerns the space, as PT Sans does for a third of English words. Offline, as a hypothesis (Node 23, Bun 1.4.2, d8 15.4.80 and the SpiderMonkey 156 shell on a stand-in Canvas where a quarter of the characters kern with the space, 2026-10-03): with the lookups the masonry demo's cards respelled in Cyrillic letters prepare seen text 11 to 12% faster than with the tests, and 29 to 34% faster with every eighth word in brackets; new text 5 to 11% and 20 to 26% faster. With the tests those two had read 24% and 68% slower than before #408, where the cards as they are read 10 to 13% and 20% slower (Node 23, 2026-10-02), so part of the kerning's cost on such text stays, in the reads themselves. A bench row of Cyrillic text in PT Sans would settle how much.
- In Chrome, a list made by a function that runs once a text and filled by a compiled loop in another function can slow every later `push` in that loop for the page's life: V8 holds a list from `[]` as small integers until the function that made it has run about eight times, and a compiled `push` of anything else onto such a list is deoptimized once and never compiled inline again (RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, JavaScript Engines, under A list made where it is filled). `measureAnalysis()` makes a text's lists itself for that reason: made by its caller, in a loop that pushed, they cost Chrome 5-11% of preparing the bench's long texts. A rich-inline paragraph's lists are made by `prepareRichInline()`, so the loop in `measureAnalysis()` stores each segment at its index, in a text's lists as in a paragraph's, and pushes nothing: V8 compiles a store by index with the list's change of form in it and the code stays compiled (V8's source and its shell, a hypothesis). On the one page order found to cost pushes, a page of mostly plain text whose first rich paragraphs are short, Chrome 154 prepares plain text again 4-9% faster with the stores than with pushes (two foreground runs of five sessions, 2026-10-08). What stays open is the store's cost and what the bench can see. Against a push, a store costs every text about 1% of preparing it again in Safari 27: 0.5-1.3% of Latin, CJK and mixed messages measured before, whichever build the bench took as its base, and up to 1.9% in one run, above the push in 120 of 140 readings, 14 entries that prepare text again in two runs of five sessions, none of which the bench calls slower; and 0.6-0.8% of CJK messages in Chrome 154 (2026-10-08). Against main, `prepare()` and one `layout()` of CJK messages measured before read 1.3% slower in Chrome, in each of 16 foreground sessions, and in Safari, in 15 of 16 (2026-10-09), where the build with pushes read 0.3% and 0.2% slower over five (2026-10-08).
