The other route is a paragraph's lists made inside `measureAnalysis()`, as a text's are, and pushed onto: a text then pays for no store, rich text prepared again read about 1% slower with it in Safari and Chrome, and it takes 10 lines of code more than the stores, or 6 in a form no browser has timed. Not timed either: a text's lists made by its caller with the stores, five lines of code shorter. The choice reopens if a store's cost in Safari grows past what the bench calls, and the stores can be pushes again if V8 comes to compile a `push` inline after it has failed there once (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-09, a paragraph's lists). The bench has no document with such an order of texts, nor a fresh page whose every width is a whole number, where Safari 27 runs the line functions at a faster level only while every list they read holds integers, worth 13-23% of counting, walking and stepping through the lines of a page of CJK rich paragraphs, for 10% more to prepare them again (by the medians of eight fresh pages a build, not a paired figure; 2026-10-09): both were timed in a copy of the bench that isn't checked in, so a bench run doesn't catch a later change that pushes onto a list made outside the function that fills it, or one that puts a fraction into every paragraph's lists.
- Since the one-paragraph design (RESEARCH.md, Rich Inline Boundaries, Rich Inline As One Paragraph), rich inline is slower than main at #459 on CJK text in three places. They show on documents the bench doesn't hold, so no bench run sees them: the bench's CJK messages as rich paragraphs, in three documents (every paragraph one item; the shapes of the Markdown chat demo's paragraphs, 86% of them one item, the mixed page; and the shapes of its styled paragraphs alone), each with a twin cut down to text whose every width is a whole number of pixels (two foreground runs of five sessions a browser, 2026-10-09; Chrome 154.0.8037.98, Firefox 156.0.1, Safari 27.0; that section, under Speed, has the timing and what the same documents gain):
  - Firefox's `walkRichInlineLineRanges()` is 6.1% slower on one-item paragraphs where every width is whole, which the bench calls slower in both runs, and 3.7% slower with ordinary widths, in each of ten sessions and under the 5% floor of the bench's rich row: about 0.2 µs per 1,000 units either way. `walkPreparedLinesRaw()` calls its visitor at one place, preparation hands it one visitor, to find a paragraph's whole line (`findWholeLine()`), and the walk another, and Firefox inlines a callee only where a call has had one, so the walk's visitor is a call a line (RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, JavaScript Engines, under The walker's visitor call).
  - Safari's `layoutNextRichInlineLineRange()` is 8.4% slower on the mixed page, in each of ten sessions and called slower in one run of the two, and 8.2% slower on its whole-width twin, in eight of ten: JavaScriptCore compiles the walkers for the calls preparation makes of them, and the calls of the full walker that step a line fail that code's entry check and run in the baseline tier (the same entry).
  - Safari's `prepareRichInline()` is 7.5% slower on styled paragraphs it has measured before, in each of ten sessions and not called slower, and 4.1% slower on their whole-width twin, in seven of ten. The cause is found in part: a paragraph there is a segment a character, and where each segment's text sits in its item, which a paragraph of several items records as it is prepared, is 3.7% of that preparing in JavaScriptCore's shell, a hypothesis; the rest isn't located (RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, JavaScript Engines, under A paragraph's sparse lists made whole).

  Two changes together would cure the first two, and were measured and left out: preparation finding a paragraph's whole line by one step, with no visitor, and the count of lines walking with a loop of its own. Written as ordinary code they cost Chrome a few percent of counting lines, plain text's too; the form that Chrome and Firefox read faster than that one writes one rule out twice; and either adds 21-24 lines of code for costs of about 0.2 µs per 1,000 units. It reopens with a form that is plain and costs no engine, with a pinned browser that moves the cost, or with a real page where walking or stepping through such paragraphs matters at this size (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-09, a loop of its own for the count of lines). Not traced: two more of Safari's readings on those documents, above main in each of ten sessions and under the floor, `walkRichInlineLineRanges()` on the mixed page, 3.4%, and `layoutNextRichInlineLineRange()` on styled paragraphs, 2.9%.
- Two more places where rich inline as one paragraph reads slower than main at #459, on text the bench holds (foreground, 2026-10-09; RESEARCH.md, Rich Inline Boundaries, Rich Inline As One Paragraph, under Speed, has the timing). On the bench's stress items, a word or a space each, Safari's `prepareRichInline()` with one `measureRichInlineStats()` of what it returns, which is what the bench's entry times, is 12% slower on text it has measured before, above main in 15 of 16 sessions, called slower in one run of four and not over the 16, and since that count alone is 76% faster there, preparation alone loses more than the entry reads; new stress items read 2-4% slower in the three browsers, in 10, 10 and 13 of 16 sessions, a size the bench can neither call nor rule out on new text. On stress items measured before, the design's first build read 25-38% slower in Safari, which JavaScriptCore's shell put on the slices of a paragraph's joined text (a hypothesis); what is left reopens with a way to prepare a paragraph that doesn't slice its joined text, or if it shows in an app (RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-06, rich inline is one paragraph). And on the plain worst-case rows that run the full walker, whose tests for a paragraph sit on a text's path: Firefox's `layout()` of text dense with soft hyphens is 3.6% slower, in each of six sessions, and Safari's `layout()` and `walkLineRanges()` of pre-wrap chunks 3.0% and 3.2% slower, in five and six of six, each of the three called slower in one run of two and none over the six (RESEARCH.md, Keeping Work Bounded, Work Done Only Where A Rule Applies, has what those tests cost a text and the restructurings measured and left out).

### Small ones

- On `zh` pages, and pages without a language under a Chinese UI, Chrome keeps `£€£€` of `£€£€““tail` on the first line, where the Chromium profile ends it after `£€£`; the scan agrees with Chrome, so the widths differ. Not traced (98 cases; 9 / 0 / 0; 2026-09-23).
- Under keep-all, Safari offers no break on either side of NEL, and the WebKit scan none after `-` in Latin text; recheck in installed Safari (by 2026-09-12, Safari 26.5.2).
- Firefox keeps `x`, U+2028, U+202F on one line, where Pretext gives U+202F its own; not traced, since the offline widths can't measure U+202F there (26 cases; 0 / 4 / 0; 2026-09-23).
- Chrome and Safari never end an emergency line with an opening bracket after emoji or digits (`12(ab`), where Firefox and every profile do. Not a case (2026-09-23).
- Letter-spaced Shantell Sans is 0.016px wider than Canvas with `letterSpacing` set, likely because ligatures turn off (by 2026-09-12).
- Unexplained: Amiri `il` at a line start measures 2.544px or 7.416px in Safari (by 2026-09-12, Safari 26.5.2).
- The README's advice for a user's minimum font size, to measure a `font-size: 1px; line-height: 1` element and pass Pretext the larger size, names Chrome and Firefox and falls short in Safari, which scales a px `line-height` with the size: with WebKit's minimum at 14px, 12px/18px text paints at 14px in 21px lines, where Chrome and Firefox keep 18px lines, so there `layout()` needs the painted line's height too (webkit-host with the preference set, 2026-10-01; that Safari's "Never use font sizes smaller than" setting sets that preference was read, not run). Text-only zoom has no advice at all (RESEARCH.md, Measurement Model).
- The Blink scan makes one ICU pass per text, where Blink restarts ICU at each line start without context, so LB20a applies there (`a‐b` under break-all and loose line breaking: `a` / `‐b`); the two differ in 86 of 188,274 verdicts (the emulation study, 2026-09-15 to 24; RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Chrome). Blink's own rule for a hyphen before a digit reads the same start, and is followed (#NNN): the scan marks its break, and a line that starts at the hyphen, as after a word cut right before it, has none there. ICU's half of that shape stays, under `word-break: normal` too: a hyphen that starts a line stays with a letter after it that ICU decides, one above U+007F after `-` or any letter after U+2010, where the scan, which read the text before the hyphen, breaks after it. In 16px Arial at 28px, `aaa-éééé` is `aaa` / `-éé` / `éé` in Chrome and `aaa` / `-` / `ééé` / `é` in the profile, a wrong height, and `aaa‐bbbb` with U+2010 likewise (`bun harness explain --text=…`, Chrome 154.0.8037.98, 2026-10-10). No real-usage case has a line of Chrome's that starts so; 6 catalog cases and 2 rich ones do, Hebrew, Thai or Arabic letters after `-`, 7 of them under 24px, all accepted. A port needs ICU's boundaries from each line's start on, where a line starts inside a word: not built.
- A `maxWidth` of `NaN` is checked by no stream. The line APIs called once for a paragraph lay it out as an unbounded width (`normalizeMaxWidth()`, #401); `layoutNextLine()`, `layoutNextLineRange()` and `layoutNextRichInlineLineRange()` are called once for each line, and with the comparison in them Chrome's mixed stream row read 1.4-11.2% slower in each of five sessions; with the check in the clamp each line loop already makes, which covered the streams, Firefox's mixed line and resize rows read 1.7-10.2% slower in each of three (#409, closed; RESEARCH.md, Decisions Log, 2026-10-02). A stream always returns at such a width. Each admission asks whether a segment overflows the line, which nothing does at `NaN`, so the lines break as at an unbounded width. Three places ask whether something fits, which nothing does either, and there a streamed line differs from the one at `Infinity`:
  - A pre-wrap line that ends, before a newline or the text's end, in spaces, or in tabs where the profile hangs them (`hangTabs`: Blink's and WebKit's, not Gecko's), reports a width of `NaN`, in plain text and rich inline: what hangs is clamped to the available width with `Math.min` (`walkPreparedComplexLines()`). A Gecko-profile line that ends in a tab counts the tab and reports its width. The line's range is the same.
  - In the Blink and Gecko profiles on desktop, a line that starts inside a segment holding an invisible character, such as a ZWNJ or a word joiner, ends after one grapheme, where at `Infinity` it takes the rest of the text. An earlier line at a real width leaves such a start. It differs only from a start whose widths to the segment's end were measured apart (the segment's entry geometry has an entry for that grapheme), since `getFreshLineEnd()` (`src/entry-geometry.ts`) asks whether they fit; other starts in such a segment take the rest.
  - Rich inline takes a paragraph whose whole width fits as one line and walks it otherwise (`findWholeLine()`, `src/rich-inline.ts`), so at `NaN` it walks every paragraph.

  Of 5,932 plain and 2,068 rich cases drawn from the sets, on the stand-in Canvas in each of the four profiles, the first differs for 15-17 plain and 35 rich cases, the second for 94 and 103 plain and 8 and 46 rich in the Gecko and Blink profiles, and the third for 9 rich in the Gecko profile; no other difference showed, and the six APIs called once for a paragraph differ for none (2026-10-02). An app that streams before its container is measured can pass `Infinity`. Whether `NaN` throws instead, as a `letterSpacing` that isn't finite does (#356), is on the API discussion's list (TODO.md).
- A start cursor that is no position in the text is checked by no stream. In `layoutNextLine()` and `layoutNextLineRange()` a segment index of `NaN` never returns on text with a soft hyphen, nor `-Infinity` on any text; -1 gives a line that starts with `undefined`; a grapheme index past its segment's end, as a cursor kept from a longer text can be, gives a line of width `NaN`, and one inside a space throws a `TypeError`. `layoutNextRichInlineLineRange()` never returns from an item index of `-Infinity`. And `measureLineStats()`, `walkLineRanges()` and `layoutWithLines()` never return on a JSON copy of a handle, as the walkers count segments to the length of a typed array, which the copy lacks (CHANGELOG, #340). Fixes for both are on branch `plain-hardening` (`0c0ad8d5`, `ff3576f5`, `27c1f6a0`), which stays unmerged and undeleted while this entry points at it. They were left out of #401 for what they cost in the bench: the two cursor checks made Chrome's mixed stream row 5% and then 11% slower, and counting segments by `widths` made Safari's pre-wrap layout 3.6-5.1% slower (2 sessions each, Chrome 154.0.8037.57 and Safari 27.0, 2026-10-01). A form that costs those rows nothing reopens them, or the maintainer's word that the checks are worth the cost.

### Never measured

- Whether a line or piece measured with Canvas matches its painted DOM width in installed browsers, the premise of painting one element per line, as the Markdown chat does. Three shapes are known not to in the pinned browsers: the text of a line that ends at a chosen soft hyphen, in Chrome a line that ends in a closing mark halved at the wrap (both under Deferred engine decisions), and a line that holds a soft hyphen it doesn't end at (Line edges). No check paints lines one by one, so others may exist.
- The checks from #321's decision 7 that the per-engine rebuild didn't settle: Chrome's whole-string widths under `optimizeLegibility`, held-out Chrome cases at a real DPR 2, and the U+2010 width in Amiri and Noto Naskh Arabic.
- Whether an iPhone holds 100k chat messages now (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, The Markdown Chat At Scale): offline on 2026-09-16, the branch that became #340, plus three memory cuts that never landed, retained 565-625 MiB in JavaScriptCore, against 858 MiB for main before #340.

## Deferred engine decisions

- Lam + alef when an overlong Arabic word breaks: Canvas can't tell whether a font draws it as one cluster, so every default is a guess (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends). No change now; decide if main ever breaks overlong Arabic words by cluster. Reopens when `getTextClusters()` or `TextMetrics.advances` ships.
- Per-paragraph contexts in the Gecko profile, against Firefox's late family names (Contexts kept for the page's life).
- The text of a line that ends at a chosen soft hyphen: it ends with `-` (`src/line-text.ts`; README, hyphenation), where the browsers paint U+2010 where a font has it (Line edges). Since #396 every profile's line width counts the hyphen its browser paints, so an app that paints `line.text` itself draws a line 1.6px wider than its reported width in 16px Inter, whose U+2010 is 5.73px and whose `-` 7.36px, and about 11px narrower in 16px Songti SC, whose U+2010 is 16px. In most fonts the browser's hyphen is as wide as `-`: its line differed from the one `-` gives in 19 of 129 font lists tried in webkit-host, 24 in Chrome and 31 in Firefox, mostly Inter, Chinese fonts and, in Firefox, lists whose first family has no U+2010. In 520 paragraphs of soft-hyphenated prose in 16px Inter at 120-600px, 47 of the 792 lines that end at a soft hyphen fit only with U+2010, so a painted `-` passes the paragraph's width there, by up to 1.54px (webkit-host, 2026-10-01). Before, text and width agreed and both missed the browser's line. The choices: leave it and say so in the README; end the text with the measured hyphen, which changes what the README promises and what an app that looks for a trailing `-` finds; or measure `-` again, which gives up the browser's breaks in those fonts. Until the API discussion settles it (TODO.md), the text keeps `-`.
- A line that ends in a closing mark Chrome halves, painted alone. Under `text-spacing-trim: normal` Blink sets a fullwidth closing bracket at half width where a line ends after it and doesn't fit otherwise (`ShapingLineBreaker::ShapeLine`, `shaping_line_breaker.cc:342-363`, for the marks of `Character::MaybeHanKerningClose`, `character.cc:130-135`, which leaves out `。` and `、`; Chromium 153), and the Chromium profile's line width counts that (`lineEndTrims`, `src/han-kerning.ts`). Text that doesn't wrap never reaches `ShapeLine` (`LineBreaker::HandleText`, `line_breaker.cc:1392-1497`, takes the item whole), so the same line in a `white-space: nowrap` or `pre` element, as the Markdown chat paints one, is half an em wider than its reported width: `もう一度確認します。小さい「っ」` in 16px `"Helvetica Neue", "PingFang SC", "Apple Color Emoji"` is 248px as the first line of a paragraph 248px to just under 256px wide, and 256px alone. No line moves, and the paragraph's own lines, breaks and widths agree: in 456 layouts of 12 Japanese and Chinese paragraphs at 150-557px in that font list, 32 of 1,765 lines end so, each 8px wider alone and past the paragraph's width by 0.48-8px; 31 of 1,662 in 15px PingFang SC, 31 of 1,767 in 16px Hiragino Sans, and none of 1,786 in Songti SC (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, 2026-10-04). It takes a font with `halt`, as PingFang SC, Hiragino Sans and Hiragino Mincho ProN have and Songti SC, Heiti SC, Kaiti SC and Arial Unicode MS don't, and one of the brackets 」 ） 』 】 》 〉 ］ ｝ 〕. Firefox 156.0.1 and webkit-host take no `text-spacing-trim` value, don't trim and have none of it. The extra is the mark's blank right half: a start-aligned line's glyphs sit where the paragraph's do, and what shows is a background or underline on the piece that ends the line (a link's box 128px for 120px), a line the browser aligns to the end (glyphs up to 7.5px off), and an ancestor that scrolls sideways (4px more scroll width at 252px). No `text-spacing-trim` value changes the painted line: Chrome takes `normal`, `space-all`, `space-first` and `trim-start` (`text_spacing_trim.h:12-39`), none reaches text that doesn't wrap, and `space-all` also undoes the pair trims the width counts. Two things do. The last character in a span with `font-feature-settings: "halt"` matched the reported width on all 63 such prose lines of the two PingFang lists and on 384 built ones (3 of those 0.64px off with the halt as without, by Hiragino Mincho ProN's kerning between kana), but only a trimmed line may take it, since a halted mark that wasn't trimmed leaves the line half an em short; the line's text prepared alone measuring wider than the line's width told all 94 trimmed lines of the three lists from the rest, which the width alone can't. Or the line's element wraps at the paragraph's width (`white-space: normal` or `pre-wrap`), where Chrome trims for itself: none of 6,980 lines wrapped, though any other width the two disagree on would wrap one. A box as wide as the reported width, placed by Pretext's numbers, puts an end-aligned line's glyphs where the paragraph has them (the two PingFang lists). This is the soft-hyphen question above from the other side: there the text is Pretext's to change, here the browser's shaping depends on the wrap, so only the line can say it. The choices: leave it and say so in `pages/demos/markdown-chat.md`; or have a line, and a rich line's last fragment, report the trim it took at its end, which the walker holds (`lineEndTrimmed`, `src/line-break.ts`), for the painter to halve that mark, with a check that paints lines one by one to guard it. It belongs with that question in the API discussion; until the maintainer settles it, nothing changes. Not looked at: the trim right before a padded span's end, a right-to-left paragraph holding such text, a font whose curly quotes are fullwidth, where Blink would trim `”`, and Windows' and Android's fonts.
- An Arabic-script soft-hyphen policy, only once demand for Persian appears, and after observing how browsers render soft hyphens typed in place of ZWNJ (RESEARCH.md, Break Opportunities From Engine Data, has how rare they are).

## Harness debt

What each blind spot in harness/README.md, "Bounds and blind spots", would take, and the debt it doesn't list:
- The hyphen drawn at a soft-hyphen break: recordings that tell it apart, and every case recorded again.
- The real-usage sample has no blank line and no tab in its 3,335 pre-wrap draws, so a planted tab-stop defect left the headline unchanged: give it a sourced share of such text.
- Several rules of the Gecko profile's analysis of bidi controls (#368): a planted defect that gives a soft hyphen after a run's last control a break passes `bun test` and Firefox's `check`, and only probes that aren't checked in see it; ones that leave the break after a run at a chunk start, skip rich inline's trim of a trailing control, or a chunk start after a hard break pass Firefox's `check`, and only `bun test` sees them. Since #399 Firefox's `check` fails where the white-space run doesn't read past a bidi control or a soft hyphen: the facts set holds `ab`, space, LRM or RLM, space, `cd` in both paragraph directions, and the catalog space, LRM, space, `ab cd`. A run that drops its space where it goes on into trailing white space, as in a rich item ending in space, soft hyphen, LF before `this word`, passes Firefox's `check`, and only `bun test` sees it; that item as a rich template also fails 2 Chrome and 3 webkit-host cases, for their own soft hyphens at item edges (Rich-inline item edges). No catalog text starts with white space, a control and a line feed, where a collapse that kept the first space lost 78 probe cases. No checked-in case holds a CR or FF beside a dropped character and white space, so a run that reads through one, in a text or past a rich item's end, passes Firefox's `check`, and only `bun test` sees it; where one takes a space's room, 9 catalog and facts cases fail. Catalog templates recorded in all three browsers would guard the rest: space, LRM, LF, `ab cd`; `abc`, LRM, soft hyphen, `def gh`; a letter-spaced leading control; a rich item ending in space, LRM; and `ab`, space, LRM, CR, LF, `cd`.
- HanKerning's types from a font's ink (`getTypeFromBounds` and `getFontData`, `src/han-kerning.ts`): planted defects that never type a mark as a middle from its bounds, compare a group's glyphs without their advances, compare a mark's ink with half its em strictly, type the four dots without `、` (`group(1, 4)`), or call the quotes fullwidth by the single ones alone pass `bun test`, whose fake fonts draw no colon centered next to Han text, no `、` alone across its em and no single quotes alone an em wide. Chrome 154 types the centered colon and semicolon of 16px Hiragino Sans under `ja` as middles: each halts `「` after it and `」` before it and is never halted itself (2026-10-01). A unit-test font with a centered colon, or a catalog template of `：「` and `」：` in that font, would guard the first; a unit-test font of each of the other two shapes, the last two.
- An item's fresh-line geometry in a rich paragraph (`src/entry-geometry.ts`; `prepareRichInline()` copies each item's to the paragraph's segments), under the Chromium profile: a planted defect that leaves it out passes `bun test` and the offline invariants in every profile and fails no Chrome case that passes, and Chrome's `check` blocks only on an accepted case that then passes, the one at 44px of the rich set's `item-edges/padded-joiner-start`, whose failure is that geometry's own gap (Rich-inline item edges); Firefox's `check` fails 2 catalog cases of `rule/zwnj`, both under 24px (2026-10-10).
- The branch of `createLine()` (`src/rich-inline.ts`) that takes what a line's width leaves out from a fragment's gap: a planted defect that never takes from a gap passes `bun test` and the offline invariants in every profile, and changes no checked-in case's layout on the stand-in Canvas (2026-10-10).
- A rich item's trailing white space that Firefox keeps as a box at a line's end: the scorer gives an item's trailing white space to no line, as it is the gap of the fragment after it (`alignStream`, `harness/predict.ts`), so a prediction with Firefox's breaks fails where the next item starts with characters of no width that end the line, as items `Hi`, space and LRM, FF, `there you` (40 probe cases at 12-58px, 2026-10-01; none checked in). A rule that gives that white space to the line the item's last unit is on would see them, checked as #399's rule for white space inside a text was (harness/README.md, What a case is and when it passes).
- The offline invariants (`harness/invariants.ts`) don't ask for a gap where only what the engine profile's analysis takes out of the text stands between two fragments, a lone CR in the WebKit and Gecko profiles and an FF in the Gecko profile, and nothing in them forbids one there: a build that lays a lone CR at an item's edge out as a space passes them, on the rich set's carriage-return cases too, and only the recordings hold the rule (such a build passes 25 of webkit-host's 41 cases of the first three templates of the set's `item-edges/carriage-return` family and the first of its `item-edges/separator-before-space`, recorded 2026-10-07, where the rule passes 38 and the three at width 1 are accepted). A check that the later fragment has no gap, where only such characters and no line feed stand between the two, would hold it.
- Not traced: on a rich item that starts with a space and an LRM after another item (`see`, then an item of a space, LRM and `this word`), the offline invariants' check of a gap's width fails in the Blink profile and in the profile for engines Pretext doesn't recognize. The library's gap holds a kerning between the space and the text beside it that the check doesn't expect there: 186 failures over the 11,878 cases of three probes that aren't checked in, the same list whichever item the white space after a CR belongs to (2026-10-07, on the stand-in Canvas). No checked-in case holds the shape, so `gate` passes, and which width Chrome draws wasn't looked at.
- `equal` in webkit-host on two real-usage draws of Ethiopic text under system font lists (`sample-19fdc5774f8f01ab`, `-apple-system`; `sample-6ca83f78f802bc2a`, `system-ui`): their line widths differ between a tree and itself, in one draw or both, in most runs, whole set or the two alone (2026-10-01), so a width difference there is the browser's state between the two builds it measures, not a change. Listing them in `harness/varying/webkit-host.txt` as varying between runs would stop `equal` counting them and `check` judging them.
- The Blink profile's rule for a Common character under a mark that has script extensions (Letter spacing): no recorded case holds a digit or a bracket under one under letter spacing, so the rule moved no case when it landed and only the gap matrix in `src/layout.test.ts` sees a defect in it; its five rows are Chrome 154's, Firefox 156's and webkit-host's gaps (2026-10-01). A space under one is in 7 Chrome cases of the catalog's `chromium-script-spacing`, which moved when the reader came to read a text's spaces (#408), and in one row of that test, Chrome's alone.
- Laying text out again at its own widest line: a candidate invariant.
- An emoji modifier split from its base across items: a rich case.
- A rich line that starts with a collapsible space its start removes: the scores read visible characters, and the line APIs' check compares a fragment's text only with its item's own over its cursors, so rich lines that started at the space after a line ending under negative letter spacing passed every case (2026-09-29). A check that a rich line starts where its text in one node does, over the source, would see it.
- The check of a rich fragment's text (`fragmentProblem()`, `harness/predict.ts`) fails a few fragments the library itself lays out, around a line or paragraph separator: mostly in pre-wrap, where the fragment's stretch of its item's text ends with a separator, or with a line feed, and bidi controls or a soft hyphen after it. On random paragraphs laid out on the stand-in Canvas, 8,000 a profile drawn twice, it failed 3 and 0 of some 140,000 fragments in the WebKit profile, 76 and 97 of 133,000 in the Gecko profile and 36 and 44 of 139,000 in the Blink profile (the library at 4be05bb6, 2026-10-07; the paragraphs weren't kept). Not traced, so whether the check or the fragment's place in its item is off isn't known; no checked-in case holds one.
- A new environment key starts the page-history list empty, and two orders find few of Firefox's page-history cases, so `repin` keeps the list it finds.
- Page-history cases go unscored, 182 in webkit-host and 29 in Firefox, though each has one layout in a browser process that laid out nothing before it, which is all the library can predict: 7 of 11 webkit-host cases that had been pinned to a layout after a twin were accepted failures the library gets right alone (2026-09-30). Four of the 30 webkit-host cases listed that day fail alone at 24px or wider and are on no list since: `old-gate-91674bab36f09a1b`, `old-gate-d938fc2ff2e06063` and `old-gate-0389719e61d4cfa7` (`a`, VT, `aabb((`, two Arabic letters, in 24px Noto Nastaliq Urdu at 48px and in 16px Arial at 27.86px, pre-wrap and not) and `old-gate-101f07ad5f22eefc` (two pointed Arabic letters, two closing curly quotes and `tail` at 1.5px letter spacing, right to left at 31.2px), each a shape the accepted list still holds siblings of, under pasted C0 and C1 controls, VT between Latin letters before brackets and Arabic, and letter spacing across scripts. Scoring them takes a recording per case in a process of its own, 1-2 s in webkit-host and 16 s in Firefox with its start-up hold, and a rule for what `record` does with them; open for the maintainer.
- `record`'s two opposite orders still pin a case to the layout after its twins where it has twins on both sides in the sort order: a pass that gave every webkit-host case a process no earlier case shared a text with (192 processes, 512 s) found one, `old-gate-101f07ad5f22eefc`. Such a case blocks the gate that draws it, as a stale recording does, until `record --cases` lists it. And a twin's layout shows in neither order where WebKit's cache dropped the twin's entry first (RESEARCH.md, Engine Facts, Safari (WebKit)): that case stays pinned at its layout alone, which is the one to score, until a gate draws both and lists it as page history, so the list can grow by a case on an unrelated change.
- A left-to-right paragraph laid out after a right-to-left one of the same text in one webkit-host process came out with a break the paragraph alone doesn't have: `overnight. Ordering now.` in 15px Helvetica Neue at 166px took two lines, the full stop alone on the second, and one line alone (2026-10-04). It is one more webkit-host result that depends on the layouts before it in its process (harness/README.md, Accepted and varying lists), and the two entries above are about such cases: ones that differ only in direction want separate processes or both orders.
- A pinned Chrome copy can update itself: Google's updater replaced the copy of 154.0.8037.57 in place with 154.0.8037.98 (2026-10-06), so no copy of the build under the recordings was left to run, and the harness refused every Chrome job until a repin. A Firefox copy is kept from it by a policy file inside the bundle (`pinInstalled()`, `harness/browsers.ts`); what keeps the updater off a Chrome copy isn't known.
- No planted fault, a deliberately broken copy of the code run to prove a check catches it, guards the watchdog's kill, the bench's shuffle and its separate compiles (each copy of the library compiled in a module of its own), Firefox's start-up hold, the page passing the browser's name to the recorder, or the cap on a job's browser beyond its kill needing no `ps` table; nor three checks of the offline invariants (`harness/invariants.ts`): that lines cover the source (`coverage`), that a materialized line passed back as a range gives the same line (`round trip`), and that the line APIs ask Canvas nothing after preparing.
- WebKit's whole-pixel width for a recorded line that ends in a space (harness/README.md, Bounds and blind spots): a way to read where such a line's text ends that isn't a character's box, then those cases recorded again in webkit-host and Safari.
- The accepted lists' reasons that name a shape of input and no engine rule, a fifth of Chrome's and Firefox's entries (harness/README.md, Accepted and varying lists): trace them and split each by cause when the sets are made again.
- The bench's floors are one per row for all three browsers, from one calibration of three sessions, and copies of one build sit further apart than they allow: in other runs of 2026-09-26 to 30 Safari's control held 6% from base in all of three sessions on a `lines` row (floor 1%), 13% on rich walk and 15% on a `resize` row (floors 5%), and Chrome's Thai `new` row (floor 6%) read slower in all three sessions of main against itself, so such a row can still read slower or faster on a copy's speed. Floors per browser, from a calibration of more sessions, would close it. The `lines` row's Latin and CJK entries take its 1% floor, which no calibration has fitted to them, and Arabic and Thai messages have no line rows of their own. The `rich` row's walk and stream, which keep each line they are handed, and its two chat documents take its 5% floor, fitted to the stress items with a callback that read only a line's width: main against itself wasn't called over eleven sessions, and the fitting rule gives 7% on one run of three (harness/README.md, Bench). No floor short of the distance between a copy's two speeds, about 12% on the chat documents' `rich-seen` in Chrome, covers an entry whose slow copy is the candidate's, which three sessions call on identical code once in 27 runs for each such entry (RESEARCH.md, Evaluation Traps, Timing); levelling what the copies prepare before the timed rounds might end the slow copy, and isn't tried.
- `bun harness repin` doesn't look for the two pair tables (DEVELOPMENT.md, Engine Data), so a build that changed one passes it unless a recorded case holds the pair. Chrome's table is in Chrome's framework (154.0.8037.98, 2026-10-06) and WebKit's in two files of the system's dyld shared cache, where WebKit's code is (macOS 27.0, 2026-10-05), each as its engine file gives its 6,244 bytes, so `repin` could look for them as it does ICU's property arrays.
- Whether `bun harness repin` should record a seeded sample first, and every case only if the sample differs; open for the maintainer. One pass over every case took 57 s in Chrome, 56 s in Firefox and about 300 s in webkit-host, and a repin records two (2026-09-30, at a load average near 25).
- A whole search of the catalog no longer gives the checked-in file: on 2026-10-01 (Chrome 154.0.8037.57, Firefox 156.0.1, webkit-host) it cut 1,134 cases the file doesn't hold and left out 292 it does, most in the class matrix's families, so #399's template was searched with the whole set and only its family's cases added. The facts set came back as checked in, and the rich set but for 3 cases. Not traced; `make.ts cut`'s rule that an older case comes back byte for byte (harness/README.md, Adding a case) doesn't hold for the catalog until it is. #455's facts template was searched alone, with `widths.ts`'s functions over that one template, and its 23 cases added beside the checked-in ones, so no older facts case was derived again that day (2026-10-06).
- When the sets are made again: drop templates that show nothing others don't; audit `src/layout.test.ts` against the facts set; and search each filed report in `harness/sets/exact.ts` as a template without its width. A report goes if the dedupe keeps a change from it; if the dedupe keeps none but the report still catches a failure, refine the dedupe's kinds of break instead (for Chrome, closing marks that trim and ones that don't).

## External actions

- At the end of the project, file the collected browser bugs (TODO.md): PLATFORM_BUGS.md's unfiled rows, the per-engine rebuild's pages for unfiled bugs (`rebuild/platform-bugs/pages/` on branch `rebuild-20260916`), and the candidate upstream patches for engine hot spots (RESEARCH.md, Dead Ends, Simplifications Held Back). Leave out anything reported privately and not yet triaged. PLATFORM_BUGS.md's header says how to file and how to recheck statuses.
